PLEASANT PARAGRAPTTS. THERE are some wha always feol certain they can do anything they have never tried to.

WHIM some men discharge on obligation you can hear the report for miles round. WORSE. "Jones owes me five pounds." "That's a doubtful debt, isn't it !" "No ; I wish it was."

"Well, if I have it it is pure laziness, but if you: mother has it, it is nervous prostration." HAVING "a good time" is like tobogganing in winter—for a pleasure lasting a minute you have a ten minutes' walk up a steep hill. THE RESULT.—Friend: "Has your son learned much at college ? " Father : "He has learned to spend ten times as much money as he'll ever have." THE Lady: "If you do not move on I shall whistle for the dog." The Man: "Let me sell you a whistle, mum." SUITOR (looking at picture) : "Does your sister paint !" Little brother: " Yes ; but she's finished that an' is puttin' on the powder now. She'll be down in a minute." AFTER THE WEDDING.—She : "The groom was quite cool and collected." He: "No ; he did not seem to realise that he was losing control of him. self."

DEFINED. —Mrs. Brown: "How would you define tact '?" Mrs. Jones: "I should say that tact is the ability to make your husband believe he is having his own way." HE; "I think Dr. Jenkins will very soon have a large practice." She : " Why ?' He "He has just had a case in which he prescribed millinery for hysteria."

"HAVE PATIVICE !"—A country yokel, about to be married, arrived at the church rather earlier than the stated hour. Thinking the bride a long time, be became agitated, and commenced pacing up and down the vestry. The clerk, seeing his discomfiture, whispered to him—" Have patience, man, have patience." "Have Patience? Not I!" blurted out the bridegroom. "I came for Sal, and I'll have Sal," thinking that the clerk referred to his future sister-in-law, Patience by name. "JOHN," said his loving spouse, "I wish you would sing two or three lines of some song for me." "Whatever do you want me to do that for?" "There is something I want you to bring home, and I've completely forgotten what it is, but I think I shall remember it if you'll sing." The good-natured husband complied, and his charming but plain-speaking wife said: "Oh, yes; I remem- ber now. It's a nutmeg-grater I want." NAPOLEON lll—spoke the French of an educated German, so that a -French officer, in reporting a speech of his majesty, applied it to the phonetic system invented by Balzac in one of his novels. An amusing story is told in this connection. The Emperor felicitated Bismarck upon his French. "Monsieur de Bismarck, I have never heard a German speak French as you do." "Will you allow me to return the compliment, sire?" "Certainly." "Well," replied the then Chan- cellor, "I have never heard a Frenchman speak French as you do." To AVOID SusmaioN.—One of the most ingenious devices of a young married couple who were on their bridal tour was described by a friend of theirs. " You see," he remarked, "John and Mary wanted to have no fuss made. Mary is very quiet, and was afraid that everybody they met would be sure to know they were just married. So what do you think she did ? " " Haven't the faintest idea," the present writer replied, though he was amused and interested. "Why, they borrowed a two- year-old baby from a friend, so as to avoid sus- picion "

NOT HIS FArrix.—An Irish labourer, who was somewhat new to his work, was ploughing one day, and the furrows being uneven, the farmer told him to look at something at the other end of the field as a guide. " That cow by the gate," said he, "is quite opposite us. Now, work straight for her. "Right you are, sur," said Pat. Coming back, later on, the farmer was quite horri- fied to find that the plough had been travelling zig-zag all over the field. "How is this?" said he. " What have you been doing ?" "Shure, was Pat's reply, "I did what you told me. I worked roight straight for the cow, but the cray- chur didn't kape still." A POOR woman who kept a small shop in a northern village, and who was troubled with a husband who could scarcely be considered a credit to the family, one day found herself a widow through the sudden demise of her spouse. A lady who frequently made small purchases at the shop called to see her and to offer her sympathy, though well knowing that the man's death must in a cer- tain sense come as a relief, as the wife had often suffered from his violence. She was not, however,

quite prepared for the stoical way--2in which the wife took her bereavement. Said the lady," "I am sure, Mrs. G—, you must miss your hus- band." "Well, mum, it do seem queer to go into the shop and find something in the till 1" A TOURIST travelling through Missouri on horse- back arrived at the bank of a river that had been much swollen by the rains. There was no way to cross it except by swimming; so, dismounting, he tied his clothes to the horse and drove him into the river, swimming after him: Reaching the other side, he dressed and continued on his way. Before going twenty feet, however, he came to the forks of the road, and looked round for a sign- post. There was none ; but just across the river, near the spot he had entered to swim across, he saw a board nailed on a tree. There was nothing to do but to get in and swim across again and read that sign. He swam across and, after climbing up the bank, he read the following notice : "Five dollars fine for crossing this bridge faster than a walk."

MAKING IT CLEAR —To confuse a witness is generally an easy task, and lawyers know no easier way than to make a witness explain the meaning of his words, knowing that very few people can do so without getting excited. Occasionally a victim resents this nagging and answers in a spirited and unexpected manner. A lawyer was cross-examining a young girl of rather haughty temper. She had testified that she had seen the defendant "shy" a book at the plaintiff, and the lawyer had seized on the word. "Shy Shy a book! What do you mean by that! Will you explain to the court what the word ' shy' means! The girl leaned over to the desk beneath the witness-box, picked up a law book, and threw it at the lawyer's head, who dodged just in time. "I think the court now understands the meanin of the word shy,'" g said the judge gravely ; and the girl was allowed to finish her testimony with. out further interruption.

LADIES' LETTER.

MT Dna Moan,

The millinery for the present season Is of the most attractive description, and difficult to be made; but the clever amateur will easily manage to make a simple hat or toque, if she avoids Intricate arrangements and relies more upon folds following circular lines, as those apparently care. less and irregular folds require artistic skill and experience. Most of the high-class modistes make their own shapes, and these are generally copied from French models, but there is a good choice in buckram shapes.

A very fashionable hat ia of fancy basket straw, trimmed with white chiffon, edged with narrow blue velvet, and black coq feather in a large cluster at the left side.

The stiff, shining-surfaced gauze which is called rainbow cloth by some milliners is much used for the full swathing folds which surround the crowns of 19 hats out 420 at the moment of writing, and although it requires doxterous manipulation it keeps its condition decidedly better than the thinner gauzes and chi irons.

The newest models for children are remarkable for their simplicity of cut, effezt bcig obtained by a combination of diverse colour or inlterial, or by pretty collars or revers of light cloth, lace, or silk, the mere touch of relief tint being kept principally to the bodice. Of materials there is very little of absolute novelty, and so far the fashion for plain fabrics continues. Both coarse diagonals and the finer and more silky seises are greatly in request, and these not only in the orthodox navy and black, but in all fashionable tints, such as powder and iris blues, petunia, dark heliotrope, deep stone, and one or two of the deeper greens.

Following the fashion of their elders, the skirts of girls' costumes are all gored closely at the top, and the slight fulness arranged either in two box. pleats, or in two flat ones, closely meeting in the centre, and giving the effect of a tight top. Sleeves fit closely except just at the armhole, where the plain sleeves are gathered or pleated, but tiny pouffe, tucks or epaulettes are much in favour, and are all very compact, and only set out slightly from the arm. A very pretty frock for a young lady is of dove grey cashmere. The bodice is trimmed with fine white lace insertion, and bands of white satin ribbon, edged with black bebe ribbon. The skirt is also trimmed with white satin ribbon edged with bebe ribbon. The smart type of bodice which can be worn as a blouse, or to accompany a skirt of the same mater- ial, is most useful for summer wear, and more es- pecially where mach black is worn, as a black bodice can be daintily relieved with colour, or smartened greatly with white or cream. Black surah, for instance, makes up prettily in the tucked style, with revers of coloured silk or satin, turned back, and edge passementerie, and the full vest of mousseline de sole, chiffon, or brilliaste.

A very pretty blouse is made of pale pink and blue shot glace silk, which is trimmed in vandyked points. The yoke is of white ,atin inerveilleur, finely tucked, and the waistband and collar are of black satin ribbon.

----- -- A black bodice of peon, cle soie, relieved with cream colour looks ',veil. The yoke of creamy satin is cut square and covered with a collar of 6cru renaissance lace, supplemented by small tabs of satin, edged narrow sequin gimp, and fall- ing over a frill of lace. The belt, bands on sleeve, and centre line are of 4cru insertion lace.

----- --- For those who prefer colour on a black bodice, a delicate green or salmon-pink is very effective in combination with black lace using black guipure over pink for the yoke, and the pink uncovered, and edged narrow jet or black sequins for the tabs. The frill should be of black lace, and the belt and sleeve bands of black insertion over pink bands. Grass lawn in this fashion, with cream-white silk or mery and cream-coloured lace, is most attrac- tive, and the skirt can be mounted on cream-white sateen, and made with a deep bias fluunce, put on with a heading, and edged with alternate frills of pleated grass lawn and cream lace. With open-fronted coats smart vests will now be necessary, and although the Newmarket style, in single or double breast, is still in vogue, the most favoured styles are excessively fussy and full, and show the pouched effect. Soft cream lace,_ with net top and heavy guipure, or open point de Venise edge, arranged in front of a vest, is most effective in an open-fronted coat, and extremely smart, even U the vest is simply of crepon or grass lawn.

Your loving JaNtni.

WOMEN'S CHAT."

Of the true kindness of the Queen, there are few better instann, s than her recent visit to Netlev Hospital. Out of nearly el thousand invalids, Her Majesty saw all but about a nufidred, and such a visit as this must have molted no ordinary effort, and no little self-denial, on the part of a lady about to celebrate the ottainment of her reventv-ninth year. rhP returning Royal train had to be delayed an hour, whilst the Sovereign mission of sympathy was being aclornplished to the fullest extent her strength permitted. Many of my readers may not be aware that there are ce.tain seasons for cheese, as for game, but the fact re- mains. The reign of Stilton is from November to June. Cheddar is in season from February to October, and April hails the coming of the new Rognefort. From May to September Bondons, Port Saint, Gournay, NeurchAtel, Mont Oore and cream cheeses may all he served. From October to April Brie appears. As for Gruj ore, Roguefort Parmesan, and Dutch cheeses, these are perennial joys, for one may have them at any time and do no wrong. The manufacture of the richer and more expensive brands of cheese which is carried on for the most part in Europe, is an exceedingly interesting process, requiring an immense amount of skill and patience. The art of makir.g certain kind, has never been successfully imita- ted, and the natives of certain parts of the Continent of Europe therefore still retain a sort of monopoly of the trade. The most expensive cheese manufactured is the truffle cheese, which is imported from France. This cheese, as its name suggest, is made of truffles, and is worth about 45. a pound. Mix the cornflour with a little Water, boil the remainder of the water, and add it, then bail again for five minutes, stirring all the time. Add the strained piece and grated rind of the lemon, with the sugar, then pour into a basin that has been rinsad with cold water. Turn out when cold, and pour over it a small quantity of orange or lemon marmalade. 't MA DOE."

CHILDREN OF CUBA: A TALE OF LOVE AND WAR

BY GRAHAM REDPATH, Author of "The Baffled Press Gang," "When Things Went Wry," &o.

CHAPTER I. LOVIHWAKING AND LEAVE-TARING.

Two lovers stepped forth from a picturesque Spanish mansion, on to the terrace, upon which the moon shed a soft, silvery light. A suggestion of age was given to the structure by its inzr"- solidity ; but this defect had been cleverly correc:., by the addition of light verandahs, now clothed with vines and flowering creepers.

Beneath the balustraded terrace, which the whis- pering lovers crossed, stood many marble figures, almost ghostly in the mystic light. The trees cast long shadows over the lawns and upland pastures, where shadowy cattle lazily grazed. A pathway led to a thick belt of trees on the far side of Cardonnavilla estate.

As Leon and Castilla wandered through the meadow land towards their favourite haunt for the last time, their eyes eagerly scanned each detail of the scene. Silence reigned supreme, and reverie possessed the souls of those whose brightest dreams had just been suddenly shattered.

In any circumstances, parting causes pain; and the disagreeable sensations felt at different times only differ in degree. But when parting means that the broad heaving bosom of the Atlantic is to interpose between those whose affections are strong and undying, it is a trial nigh as sharp as the sting of death itself. The world is changed to those so separated ; it is another kind of life alto- gether, the single hope of meeting again possessing them.

But these lovers have now reached their bower— s seat screened by the branches of an oak. Castilia took her name from the finest province in all Spain. She was of stately form, with oval, sun- stained face, regular features, and large eyes. Her forehead was curtained with tiny waves of jetty hair—a picture of grace, gentleness, and generosity. Her tall, dark, square-set lover was a soldier, whose eyes, whilst dreamy to sadness in repose, flashed with brightness and ouiet wit at times.

Beyond them a streamlet wound its way through a vista of trees into the light, until it reached the upper lake, whereon the moonbeams danced. "Leon," said Castilia tremulously, as she broke the spell of silence, "this is our last meeting for a long, long time. "Yes, dearest. Well? " assented Leon rather despondently. "Well, you'll not forget me when I am so far away, will you ? " It was an effort for her to be so plain, to voice this tiny doubt whispered in her heart by Cupid, or his plaguing cousin Jealousy, and tear-drops gathered in Castilia's eyes. Leon, admiring her frankness, caressed her fondly as he replied: "Never, never, my darling; I pro- mise you. How could I? Long ago I learnt to love you, and every day since I have learnt to love you more. It is tearing my heart in twain to think of this separation, for it may mean more than we can tell—more even than we dare think. All the pleasantest and happiest moments of my life have been at Cardonnavilla ; all my hopes and aspirations are bound up in you ; and yet you ask me if I shall forget you now that you are going from me!" "Shall you remember me always, when I'm iii far-away Cuba, I had intended to say ? " said Castilla, as she observed Leon's abstraction.

"Shall I Le :,ble to think of aught else, you might have said," he rejoined, by way of sweet complaint. "I'm sure I shall not I " Then Castilia held up her pretty face coquettishly, and smiled her thank- fulness for the words, which were as dear as life to her.

Ashamed of his melancholy in face of Castilia's heroism, Leon stood up and, pointing to a tree dark against the sky, said softly: "Why, we have grown up together, and as firmly as yon vine clings to that oak do our hearts cling to each other." Fully reassured, Castilia led her lover on step by step, and so the old, old tale of love was repeated as they strolled back, by the way they had come in silence, to the terrace, where Leon took his last fond farewell. And with tear-filled eyes Castilia watched his stalwart figure diminish and disappear in the shadows of the avenue which led to her dear old home.

Half consciously she raised her eyes to look at the starry skies, where glittering worlds seemed to twinkle in sympathy with her sorrow. Calm con- templation of what we do not understand is solacing, and it soothed Castilia's heart-ache. "We must trust the Ruler of the Stars," she thought, "for to-morrow we begin a new life, about which we know as little as we do about the heavens."

A cheery laugh startled her as she entered tl“. vestibule, and the merriment of her younger sister, a pleasant, wayward girl, promptly banished her thoughts. Blue eyes and flaxen curls made Coria look more girlish than she really was. A round, coy face, with feeble chin, was hers. Her levity seemed ill-timed, and yet Castilia had not the heart to utter the rebuke ready on her tongue. "Cousin Castello is crying because we are going away," said Coria, grasping her sister's arm, and with laughing eyes adding: "Isn't it fun ? " Amusing as the situation was, Castilia repressed the ready smile, and with a sisterly kiss sent her back to console their cousin. If Cone was emotional she was also willing, and she did as she had been bidden.

Playing with the locket, cross and anchor sus- pended by a golden chain round her neck, Coria re-entered the room. These baubles had been presented to her by her sailor lad, Castello Branco. Humbled and disappointed by the sisterly ad- monition, her dimples disappeared, and her face was as inanimate and correct as chiselled alabaster. But she was a coquette by nature. Sounds of sobbing came from the corner of the room, where sat the little cadet, his face hidden in his hands. He was susceptible, kind, generous and wilful.

Coria stepped softly across the room, and her dress brushed his bowed head as she passed. ' "Ahl I knew 7ou would return soon," he said. "Yes; I didn t mean to vex you, but I couldn't help laughing. We'll only be parted for a little while, you know, and when we return to Spain we'll all be as happy and gay as ever." Castello, in an ecstasy of delight, embraced the little woman at his side, saying: "My love for you is as deep as the sea, as unquenchable as a volcano, as true as the Polar star. Ah, ray lovely cousin, it is no use—my affection for you is simply inexpressible." "Then don't try to do it, dear," she said thought- lessly ; but he took this as a taunt, for she had a teasing manner.

Glances, which tell more than the tongue can utter, and sink deeper where they strike, were quickly exchanged. Castello rose, noisily crossed the room, and picking up a book flung himself angrily into a chair.

One occupant toyed with the book, the other hummed provokingly. "When I don't talk you complain; when I do yon fret and fume. -I'm never right, always wrong. Oh, if women hail 'tint sense!" the tune seemed to say to the man. Castello's eyes lighted on De Segur s axiom: "Men say of women what pleases them; women do with men what pleases them," and then he flung down the book.

A sense of oppressive monotony and gloom was felt by those present, and through the room the moon- light flickered, bringing various objects into strant., and uncanny prominence. Sometimes a shadc. would extend till it threatened to fill the room and destroy their hopes for ever. Whenever a heavy branch of foliage swung back everything was visible in an instant, then suddenly plunged into shadow. The wind sighed in a melancholy key, for a storm was rising; and the sad hearts, as often happens, were accommodated with weird effects, as if they deserved to be helped to their fill of sorrow. Carving on furniture and wainscot assumed peculiar shapes, the uncertain light serving to con- tort and distort what were graceful curves. Boar's heads, busts, swords, shields, and breastplates were all twisted into gnome-like forms, and huge eyes seemed to ogle the disconsolate pair from every corner of the room. The many-lustred candelabra radiated and refracted a hundred gleams, like owlet-eyes, overhead. Neither was ready to lapitulate, though the stifled breathing of eithes was articulate. Suddenly something crashed in the corner of the room, and, in her fear, Coria instinctively fled tc Castello, who had risen, with open eyes and arms. Involuntarily he clasped his coauettish COUtlin,

In a moment aic sense or rear was banished. The moonlight once more streaming steadily in through the window disclosed the fact that a steel breast- plate had fallen! That was all—and yet it was sufficient to 4•lnake the troubled pair, now clasped in each other's arms; feel perfectly stupid and perfectly happy.

CHAPTER 11. RZFLECTIONS AND mcsournows.

A comfortable seat in a cosy study, with the sweet, if silent, companionship of the pipe, con- duces to a reflective frame of mind. Thus sat Dr. Jacca Cardonna,-Ithe father of Castilia and Coria. Gazing tit the shelves of books he knew so well, he was reminded of various phases of study through which he had passed, and thoughts crowded in upon him.

Resembling the Spaniard:of former times, he was country-proud, honourable, generous, and adven- turous. Fond of romance and anxious for gaiety, it was strange that a trace of austerity should lurk in the cross lines of his open countenance, where flashing eyes bespoke a kindly humour. He was a refined, scrupulous gentleman, a little rash in speculation, and somewhat blind to his own best interests, perhaps • but of late quieter and more cautious moods had swayed him.

At fifty, a new departure in life comes jarringly, and is not a pleasant subject for contemplation. Where success is by no means certain, it presents a rather gloomy side. It is a new epoch—a period of trial. _

"Everything depends upon my judgment and health—the happiness of cur home, the/prosperity of my family—and whichever way this enterprise goes, it means a renewal of our fortunes or irre- parable failure, and ruin becomes a terribly ugly word when persistently dwelt upon," exclaimed the doctor with a sigh. At Cardonnavilla it had been a happy life, and his cup of bliss had been filled to the brim. Suc- cess had smiled on him in every respect but one— the one which least of all he expected would have failed. Better let it be confessed at once, the doctor's weakness was speculation. Probably, had lie only speculated in mining—for his geological knowledge was accurate, and had proved valuable over and over again—all would have been well, and thought of the morrow been needless. But civil engineering was another thing, con- cerning which he knew nothing. There must have been something in the nature of a gambling genie generated in his brain when he decided to sink over half of his fortune in the scheme for making a canal from Brieviesca, near Burgos, to Bilboa, one of the best commercial ports in Spain. This joining of the Zbaizabal and the Ebro to provide a direct waterway to the Bay of Biscay was a great idea ; but, like many more great schemes, its greatness was its weakness. Undoubtedly it would have stimulated the now somewhat slender in- dustries of Burgos and neighbouring towns. Pay- ment of the money he owed meant ruin, and that was why the doctor determined to go abroad to retrieve his fortune, and pay back his just debts on his return. Leon and Castello alone knew of his desti-

nation, and the real reason of his going. "Are my high hopes of the future justified? Sup- posing futile efforts make my ruin more complete, will it not make our return to the land we love more and more impossible ? " He dare not think of this. _

Pertinent questions were uppermost, as he puffed rings and clouds to battle in the thick layers of smoke overhead. Fixing his eyes blankly on the shifting smoke, he viewed the main incidents of his life, an uneven, eventful life. But his nicotinous musings were eventually broken by the entr.lnce of his wife and daughters.

A good-natured, charming little lady was the doctor's wife. She has much simplicity of manner, her girlishness has not altogether gone, and obser- ving the doctor in a melting mood, probably at her half-concealed grief, she kissed her husband and sat down at his side.

Cheery commonplaces passed, but there was one common thought to all, which clogged conversation and threatened to trip up conventionalities. The tremour in the voices betrayed thought of what they did not dare to speak. "And have you been smoking all this time, father? " said Coria, for on her devolved the duty of speaking at times when there was little about which to speak. "Yes, smoking and thinking." "Do the two always go together? It must be so pleasant." "Not always, child; often, though not always. But to-night " "They did ? " "Yes to-night they did." "I thought so. I could see it in your eyes. We've left you far too long alone, and you've felt lonely." "No, no, not at all. I was thinking, and I didn't mind. Only when you laughed then I missed you; your laugh reminds me of the time when your mother was a girl." "Were you thinking about the new home in the promised land you have made us all so anxious to see ? " asked Castilia.

"No, I was thinking of myself." "Only about yourself? " said the mother. "Mostly; about you and me. Shall I tell you what it was, just as I remember it? "_

Delight was less than the listeners felt. "Well," proceeded the doctor, after laying aside his pipe, "my mind wandered back to my boyhood, when my father sat where I sit now, and I clam- bered round his knees. A tall, well proportioned soldier was he—like Leon, but broader—whom a hard life had softened. His face was furrowed with grief, and his white hairs were the result of many and early sorrows, but beneath the stern, set mouth beat a heart tender as a woman's. I was his firstborn, but my mother I never saw. That's how it happens that I spent most of my childhood in this room. Ere I was five I was fatherless as well, for his was a heavy grief. God has been more kind to me.

"At twenty-five I came from college, and went with the army into Africa, as surgeon to my father's regiment, and our colonel told me most I know of him. During the war our commander was fatally wounded, and he charged me with the care of his niece, Oliva Branco. I swore to be faithful to my trust; I don't know that I did keep it as he might have wished. Your mother can tell you that." Mrs. Cardonna smiled.

"Colonel Branco had wished that, I fancy, don't you, mother ? " interrupted Castilia. "I think he must, dear." _ _ _ _ _

"Well, I left the 'army, and I was successful as a physician, and ought to have been content. I wanted wealth; when I got that I wanted more. Only one disappointment in life have I had. I wished you girls had had a brother, someone to perpetuate our name, to be a friend to you when mother and I are gone. Men have held high office, and had titles from their king, men of chivalry and renown have fought and bled and died for their country's honour and their own, who have held your name---a name now at an end. But the bitterness is taken away, for after all, with two lovely daughters and so sweet a wife, I feel richly endowed. Besides, the day of dreams is past. I am poorer, but I am more contented." There were tears in the old man's eyes as he filled the bowl of his long pipe. Castilia held a taper, and they continued to sit and chat about domestic arrangements in their new island home, while without the rain fell, the wind shrieked, and the windows rattled, as if to make the last hours at the old home all the more memorable.

I About a classic city, where lingering relics of by- gone days are to be found, hovers the spirit of chivalry and of romance. Whether mist-clad or glazed in sunset glow, massive memorials of a distant age always charm. These multiform structures possess a peculiar fascination for a speculative and imaginative man, with his vision I fixed on the buildings before him, and his memory Icharged with the lore, legends, and customs of the past. To the decayed city of Burgos Castilian pride, prejudice, and indolence gravitate as by a natural law. They cannot restore its glory, perhaps they but deepen the gloom. Direct descendants of wealthy rulers of old Castilia love the city in its decrepitude, however, for it is here where their Lags were crowned, nnd here where the councils of state were held. High in the heavens the beautiful towers of the cathedral, one of the finest specimens of Gothic architecture in all Europe, rise sharp and clear from a confused mass of dilapidated streets, and rearing their proud pinnacles of slender stone fret- work under a canopy of empyrean blue, appear in their pride to forget the cradle of misery from which they spring. In form it bears a close re- semblance to York Minster, its exterior rich and elegant in ornament, its interior a colonnade of skilfully sculptured columns, supporting a curi- ously wrought canopy of oak. Seen in the soft light from the many-hued windows, it is well fitted to fill men's minds with pious thoughts. Burgos, standing on the higher reaches of the Douro, commands an expansive, yaried, and pleasant

_ - landscape. tioutnwards is a vast expanse of tufo variegated tableland, intersected in every direction by the tributaries of the river. Here and there yellow cornfields stand out against vast barren

tracts of naked soil, bared to the sun by the wood- man's axe; but even the fertile portion of the shews signs of neglect, through the indolent habits of the Spanish agriculturist. In the crevices and fissures of the Pararners mountains all sorts of ferns, grasses, mosses, wia lichens thrive. Below, the forest stretches over the plains. Vines climb every other tree. There are hundreds of small maize, rye, and barley patches, surrounded with thick hedges of prickly cactus and American aloe. The esculent fruit of the cactus intermingles with the luscious pomegranate. Flocks of merino • sheep and goats graze leisurely on the slopes, and in the valleys oxen wander listlessly along. Shepherds and herdsmen often sit down W smoke when their cattle halt to eat or settle down to rest. Spanish peasantry seem a silent and con- tented race.

Occasionally the quiet of the peaceful panorama is broken by the arrival of a caravan. The tread of animals echoes in the hills. Soon the mules file through a pass into the mountains and descend the rocky tracks which their shoes have cut, till they are lost to view. The sky seems always bright and clear; the pale tones merge into the azure-tinted peaks which encircle this pastoral scene of repose and plenteous- ness, and the bases of the hills are touched with purple as the evening shadows fall. Unobtrusively the drab-coloured dome of Cardonnavilla peeps out above the dark foliage of evergreen oaks. The, situation is snug and suggestive of a dignified repose. It commands a view of hill and plain, of river and valley. Unassuming architecture helps it to fit into its simple surroundings. From the lodge gates, the porch, verandah, and spear-capped dome come into view. Around the pillars and upon the walls creepers and clinging plants coil, and enhance the beautiful stonework. Over the entrance hall a helmet, spear-head, and breast-plate, on a medallion fringed with vine- leaves and grapes, shew this to be an old family seat. At either side of the inner door stands a fall suit of armour. Some busts on marble columns, amid palms and broad-leaved plants, with red and yellow flowers, stand on the marble floor. d bronze girandole, carrying dozens of oil lamps, swings from the dome. The rooms are elegantly furnished, and rare pictures of the Spanish school adorn the walls.

Early breakfasts are seldom very happy func- tions, and on people in trouble appetite seldom waits,

The Cardonnas were in low spirits, and the earlf repast on the morning of their departure was melancholy affair. Few words were spoken, an° little food eaten. Even Coria was depressed. Despair comes readily to the race, for it is palL4 of the national temperament of the Spaniard. More than once did the Cardonnas wish the!, had not lived to see the old home forsaken, and more than once did they shudder at the thought of their sojourn in the Pearl of the Antilles.

(To be continued.)

At the end of 1834, on the dissolution of the Melbourne Ministry, Mr. Gladstone was asked by Sir Robert Pee/ to accept the office ota junior Lord of the Treasury. Ad- vanceinent soon came, for in the February follow- ing he was selected for the post of Under Secretary for the Colonies. In the month, how- ever, Lord John Russell introduced a motion as to the temporalities of the Irish Church, which evoked Mr. Gladstone's vigorous opposition; but the Ministry were defeated, and Lord Melbourne again came into power. In June, 1837, came the death, of King William IV., and this led to another General Election. On this occasion Mr. Gladstone, who had now obtain et a five year? acquaintance with Parliamentary proctdure, was again returned for Newark—still in the Tory interest, let it be observed. In 1841 Lord John Russell was defeated in the House of Commons and under the premiership of Sir Robert Peel

Ma. GLADSTONE BECAME VICE PRESIDENT OP TRE

BOARD OF TRADE and Master of the Mint. In the Corn Law debates which took place in the years 1841 and 1842 he took a prominent part and it is stated that the revised tariff scheme was mainly his work, though he was opposed to Mr. Villiers, the leader of the Repeal party. In Iti43 Mr. Gladstone was made President of the Board of Trade; but after a couple of years' tenure of this office he resigned, owing to his opposition to the extension of the Maynooth Grant and the establishment of the non-sectarian colleges. In i 1846 Sir Robert Peel gave up the reffis of office, it having .become apparent that he could TM count upon the support of certain members of his Government on the ques- tion of the Corn Laws, though it was apparent that a revision was inevitable and was, in fact, impending. Llrd John Russell was requested to form an Administration, but he declined to under- take the onerous duty at this juncture, the consequence being that Sir Robert Peel returned to power. Mr. Gladstone acted in this Ministry as Colonial Secretary and eat in the House of Commons as Member for Oxford University.

VISIT TO ITALY.

Shortly after the death of Sir Robert Peet—to be precise, it was in the year 1850—Mr. Gladstone visited Naples, the outcome being the publication of those memorable letters concerning the scandalous condition of the Italian prisons. It was darling this visit that he laid the foundations of his future friendship with Cavour and Garibaldi. During this .period he severed himself from the Tories. In 1852 he joined Lord Aberdeen's Government as • Chan- cellor of the Exchequer.; but with the collapse of that Ministry after the Crimean War he was de- prived of office. Later ,on the Earl of Derby selected him as Lord High ,Commissioner to the lonian Islands. In 1859 he was again a Cabinet Minister as Chancellor of the Exchequer under Lord Palmerston. He had by this time established for himself a reputation as a master of finance, and his speeches upon the commerce of the country and kindred topics were distinguished from the ordin- ary dry-as.dust orations which had until then passed muster, for they were invested with considerable eloquence and were couched in convincing language. There was no wonder, then, that his Budgets .'were looked forward to with more than ordinary Interest. Incidentally it may be said that it was In 1861 that some sensation and not a little delight Was caused by the announcement of Mr. Gladstone that the obnoxious paper duty would be repealed. In 1865 there came a dissolution of Parliament, and In the election which ensued Mr. Gladstone was unfortunate enough to lose his seat at Oxford. He was returned for South Lancashire, however, splen- did receptions being accorded to him at Liverpool and Manchester. In the same year Lord Palmers- ton died and Earl Russell succeeded to the .Premiership, MR. GLADSTONE LEADING THE HOUSE OF COMMONS. The new Reform Bill was introduced and the formation of a "cave" in the House led to the •downfall of the Ministry. This was in 1866, and .the Earl of Derby came into power, Mr. Disraeli (afterwards Earl of Beaconsfield) leading the Wirer House. In 1867 the Derby Administration per. sformed the historic operation of "dishing the Whigs" by passing a Reform Bill. In this year Mr. Gladstone made his famous •declaration in favour of disestablishing the Irish ,Church. Early in 1868 Mr. Disraeli succeeded to the high office of Premier; but his term was corn. !paratively short on this occasion, for in the follow- ing November Parliament was dissolved. In the election which followed Mr. Gladstone lost his seat for South-west Lancashire and was returned for Greenwich. In the following year (1869) he became PREMIER FOR. TEE FIRST TIME. During the five years to 1874, when another dis- solution of Parliament occurred, many highly im- portant statutes were added to the list. It was ill this period that the disestablishnient of the Irish Church became an accomplished fact, and even while the whole of Europe was excited and in. throated by the hostilities between France and Germany the British Parliament found time and opportunity to pass the Elementary Education Act (one of the most beneficent measures of the cen- tury), the Irish Land Act, the Ab ,lition of Pur- chase in the Army, the Act for abolishing Univer- sity Tests, and the Ballot Act. The Government were defeated on the Irish University Education Bill, and Mr. Disraeli(one of the most brilliant states- men of our times and the stoutest opponent of the great Liberal leader through a long series of years) returned to power in 1874. At that time Mr. Gladstone (who was a man of 65 years of age) decided to retire from the leadership of the Liberal party and he was succeeded by the Marquess of Hartington (now Duke of Devonshire). The "Grand Old Man's" absence from the political arena was not, however, of long duration. In 1875 he emerged from his retirement to lead a great national demonstration against the Bulgarian atrocities of the Turks. In 1879 he first visited Midlothian and in the following year, at another dissolution, he was returned for the Scottish con stituency and became

===== 0002601_18980520_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
PREMIER FOR THE SECOND TIME.

Between 1880 and 1885 a large batch of useful enactments were passed. Toese ilieluded the third Reform Act, the Redistribation Act (under which was secured the most thorougo &lenge in the electoral system of the country yet made), the second Irish Land Act, the Employers' Liability Act, and so forth. In the autumn of 1885 Parlia- ment was dissolved and Mr. Gladstone was re. elected by an overwhelming majority for Mid- lothian. The Administration of the Marquess of Salisbury had not a lengthy existence, for early in 1886 the "Grand 0; to rpr.pi vor) another

summons from the Queen. On this oc(%sion he became First Lord of the Treasury and Keeper of the Privy Seal. It was in this year that the famous split occurred in the Liberal party. In consequence of differences of opinion concerning Mr. Gladstone's Irish policy the Marquess of Hartington and Sir Henry James, now Lord James (both of whom had been his cidlesgues formerly) decffned to join Ur= Cabinet, and almost immediately afterwards Mr. Chamberlain and Mr. Trevelyan (now Sir George Trevelyau, Bart.) resigned. ===== 0002601_18980520_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
lIOMB BULB BILL

in a great speech which created a sensation throughout the country, the Irish Laud Purchase Bill following a few days later. The Government were defeated by a majority of 30, this being largely attributable to the defection of the Liberal- Unionist section. The resignation of the Ministry followed, the appeal to the country leading to the defeat of Mr. Gladstone's party. The Liberal chief was returned for both Leith and Midlothian, and he chose his old constituency. In 1888 Mr. Gladstone visited Italy, and on July 25, 1889, he celebrated his golden wedding. The anniversary of his birthday in 1890 was marked by the unveil- ing of a memorial fountain at Hawarden which had been erected to commemorate the golden wedding. In 1892 another Midlothian campaign was engaged In and in the autumn of the same year the veteran leader was returned to power for a fourth time. In April, 1893, the Home Rule Bill passed the House of Commons but was rejected in the House of Lords. In the early part of 1894

MR. GLADSTONE RESIGNED THE PREMIERSHIP,

the chief cause being that he was suffering from cataract in both eyes, and on March 3 he delivered up the seals of office, Lord Rosebery succeeding him. A couple of months later a successful opera- tion was performed for the removal of the cataracts and during the summer the aged statesman appeared to get coutinuously stronger. In August, 1895, he appeared in public at Chester and made an impassioned speech on the Armenian question. It was in that year that his connection with the House of Commons (which had extended over a period of something like 60 years) terminated, for at the General Election in that year he did not seek re-election. It will be remembered that the only occasions since then on which the right hon. gentleman took part in public matters were in relation to the agitation against the Armenian atrocities.

Al A LITERARY MAN.

One of the most extraordinary features of the character of Mr. Gladstone has been his many- sidedness, if one ni,,y fitly use the expression. His fund of information on all sorts of topics has often amazed listeners, while his literary work would have served to bring an ordinary man to the front. Nothing seems to have come amiss to him, and his writings range from dissertations on theological themes to Greek transla.Dions and renderings of Latin verse. One of the most beautiful settings which proceeded from Mr. Gladstone't pen was his Latinising of the familiar hymn " Roe a of ages." As to his general literary work, it ought to be said that early in his career he published "The State In its relations with the Church,' While in 1851 his pamphlet on the Neapolitan atrocities created a great sensation. " Studies in Homer" and "Ju- ventus Mundi " followed, and amongst the other notable works which have proceeded from his pen may be mentioned "The Impregnable Rock of Holy Scripture."

===== 0002601_18980520_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
Ills 1.1021 Z

--- --- Nothing could have been more touching than the life-long devotion of \! re. Gladeznee to her distin- guished spouse. No snore grace:Ail allusion has been made to this domestic felicity than that which Mr. Justin McCarthy, M.P.. introduces into his splendidly written " Life of Giadetoue." Here it is "Without in the least, degree invading the sacred domain of a great man's private life, it may be said that no marriage could possibly be more happy than that of Mr. and Mrs. Gladstone. The pair were young together, heeaane mature together, and grew old together. Ido net mean to say that they passed their lives in the same dwelling ; but what I do mean to say is that they were always thoroughly together in purpose and in spirit, in heart and in soul. There never,could have been a wife more absolutely devoted to her husband and to his cause than Mrs. Gladstone. There was something unspeakably touching even to mere casual observers like myself, in the tender care which she always lavished upon him—a care which advancing years seemed rather to increase than to diminish. One was reminded eometimes of the saying of Burke that he never had an outside trouble in his life which did not vanish at the sight of his wife when he crossed :the threshold of his home."

To sum up: Mr. Gladstone has been a veritable Bayard in political life, for he has been truly with- out fear and without reproach. An uncompromis- ing yet generous opponent, he has ever been a loyal and sincero friend, and he has invariably been found ranged on the side of right against wrong. His eloquence was from the heart, and, though scathing in his denunciations of iniquity, there ran through the speeches a strain of high- souled appreciation of all efforts of a noble character that appealed to the political enemy as well as to the staunch admirer. A typical Briton, he has evinced the possession of the most admirable attributes of the British character, and the stern- est of his political adversaries will not willingly • grudge him the tribute of having been amongst the worthiest of the nation's sons. Let this appreciation close with the beautiful lines which Shakespeare puts into the mouth of "Gaunt" •fßichard )

I an told on what should be good authority that ithe Bishop of London has fully made up his mind Ito take action in regard to those extreme churches lin his diocese which have been specially brought lunder his notice. But it is by no means so certain 'that his efforts will be attended with success. The 'debate in the Upper House of Convocation made it clear that the bishops are still determined to adhere to their policy of " no prosecutions," and the ex- treme clergy aimed at are not all anxious to submit to the wishes of an individual bishop. It is under- stood, however, that the Bishop of London will first try moral suasion, in the hope that extreme practices may be abated. If this should fail and his formal monition be disregarded, thee he will be forced to inhibit the offending incumbent from officiating anywhere outside his own church. Such a sign of episcopal displeasure would be severely felt by many, and the moral effect would, it is be- lieved, be great. The curates will also be placed under the episcopal ban by having their licences withdrawn. This, at any rate, is what is understood in well-informed Church circles to be the Bishop's plan of campaign.

Ilwi

Acircular published here by one of the leading manufacturers of packet tobaccos, announcing that the reduced duty which came into force yesterday ,will not cause any alteration in prices,has caused a good deal ot surprise. These tobaccos, I may ex- !plain, are scarcely affected by the regulation reduc- ing the quantity of water which tobacco may con- ttain. It is the cheapest kinds, such as roll and Ishag, that will have to be drier in future. Packet tobacco has never been charged to the full amount with moisture, and the reduction in duty, as far as this is concerned, ought tog" to the consumer,after allowance has been made for the rise in leaf tobacco caused by the war. At the outside, two- pence per pound is ample to cover this, leaving four pence for the congumer,or a farthing off every ounce But this, it appears, the manufacturers have deci- ded to keep for themselves,if the public allow them. As it may be .taken that eleven million pounds of leaf approximately are used for pacerets, it follows that whot these makers propose to do is to take £lBO,OOO per year from consumera on the pretence of supplying a drier tobacco.

lAs to the effect of Mr. Chamberlain's speech abroad, there can be no doubt that it has been of the worst possible kind. lam informed to-day on what 1 believe to be good authority that an am- bassador af one of the Great Powers in London has ,in private conversation spoken very freely about ithe effect of deliverances '3 f this kind on the part of i.esponsible members of the Cabinet. If, he said in !effect, the speech were merely to be regarded as in- tended for consumption in the constituencies, it might and would be passed over, but a repetition of tspeeches of this kind could not be ignored by the !Governments of the Continent, and might eventu- ally lead to reprisals. There was even a rumour to-night that the Russian Ambassador—who is not the diplomatist to whom I have just referred—had offered an informal protest against the language employed by Mr. Chamberlain in regard to Russian iplomacy, This is possibly mere speculation, but it indicates the sense which exists of the indiscre- tion of some of the passages of the speech. Mean- while, however, the outcry which has arisen does not seem to have effected Mr. Chamberlain person- ally. It is said by his friends that he was never in better spirits or more satisfied with his own posi- tion than ha is just now.

A novel suggestion was made to Mr. Balfour by Mr. William Allan to the . effect that the Govern- ment should bring in a My to enable the Prime Minister to speak in both his Houses of Parliament. Mr. Balfour laughed at the proposal as one utterly 'ellen to the Parliamentary traditions of the country —a sentiment which was readily applauded by the i House of Commons. Later n the day, however,Mr. Allan bad the consolation of welcoming a number of belated converts to his new propaganda. Mem- bers who had paid a visit to the House of Lords to hear the Prime Minister came back declaring that they had discovered an irresistible argument in favour of the suggestion of the member for Gates- head.. Owing to the defective acoustics of the Gilded Chamber they had only caught some dis- jointed fragments of Lord Salisbury's speech. "He might might jnet as well have been speaking at a meeting of bankers," was the sarcastic comment ot one Lion member. The House of Commons is by no means a perfect auditorium, but it is immeasurably superior in that respect to the other chamber.

Recrimination between Mr. Healy and his imme- diate supporters on the one hand and the Welsh Radical members on the other was again a featuro of the resumed discussion . on the Irish Local Government Bill. At one point Mr. Samuel Evans was stung into accusing the imperturbable member for Louth of an attempt to usurp the functions of the leader of the House. Later in the evening bit- ter complaint was made by Mr. Evans, Mr. God- dard Mr. Henry Broadhurst, and other Liberal mem,bers of the persistent absense of the Liberal leader from the discussions on the Bill. As a matter f fact the was almost empty throughout the i Efouee ,nurse of the night's proceedings. Members, how- ever, remained within call.

Mr. Asquith's speech on social reforms is wel- come, and his hard-hitting sentences on the treat- ment meted out to shop assistants will be voted iwith satisfaction. " The condition of shop assis- tants," he said, " both male _and female, but more particularly the female, constituted a crying social 1 and industrial scandal, both as regarded the hours of labour,the modes of payment,the conditions under which they had their meals and slept, and indeed missed the whole of their existence, Whether inside ar outside the counter." _Those are strong words, 'but not too strong, and I loin with Mr. Asquith in his hope that a searching investigation and a well- considered agitation may speedily pave the way for ;reform.

It is to be hoped that Mr. Asquith's references to the futility of the Truck Act indicate his determin- ation, when he becomes once. more the chief power in the land on social questions, to make a clean sweep of the power of fintng and deductions. Ex- Iperience has shown that there is no safe half-way !house in this matter. The .weaker workers will re- main at the mercy of their employers so long as fining is legal. In the case of the shop assistants we have shown that the new Truck Act is abso- lutely inoperative. Mr. Asqutth's allusions to the reform of the prison systeol remind one of how reach the country owes to him for the admirable initiative, now bearing fruit, which he gave to the reform movement.

1I hear that certain eupporters who have warmly defended their Far Eastern Policy so far are 'strongly urging the Government to obtain from the Chinese Government a lease of Shanghai as well as of Wei-Hai-Wei. The former place is recommended • las being not only the chief centre of our trade with China, but also as commanding the approaches to the valley of the Yangtsa Kiang. Shangha;, it is said, has another recommendation, since it may be reached within twenty days from Liverpool by the trans-Conadian and Pacific ronte. That is, it may be so reached if the Pacific steamship services are ;accelerated.

The Money-lending Committee met on Wednes- day and agreed on the bases of their report. They were quite unanimous in regard to the recommenda- tions,which will undubtedly be of a drastic char- acter. The Chairman will now proceed to draft the !report,which will be considered at future meetings.

AMID3T WATV3L M 3

BY MR. H. WHITHAM.

I had almost written as the headline to this letter the somewhat hackneyed 'phrase, "Is civilize- a failure?" but second thougnts led me to conclude that under such a heading too much ser- monising might follow. Still the feeling is very strong within me that instead of obeyine the great Lawgiver revered by all Christians the members of the civilized world refuse utterly to "love one another," and seek only through their mental and physical abilities, how best to destroy one another. But let that pass, America is now at war with Spain, and long before this is read by my Warring- ton friends the nations of Europe may have had the opportunity, it is said they have hoped and looked for, that of seeing bow the 19th century- civilization-evolved-war-ship behaves herself. There are writers who say that the great outburst of war would have come long ere this, but that the na- tions of Europe were afraid lest the iron demons they have raised should after all prove but mon- sters created only for self-destruction. I can fancy that in Warrington there are plenty of well informed people who may ask with some I show of impatience, " Well but what is it all about, I what is America fighting with Spain about, and what does she expect to get out of a poor bankrupt nation like that?' This would be a pertinent ques- tion. Judging from such English papers as, so far, have come to hand, English readers have had scant I opportunities of knowing the great interests America has in the welfare of Cuba. Not merely pecuniary interests are involved, but humanity has, for at least three years, cried aloud for interven- tion in the affairs of desolated Cuba. Since the days of the Armada the haughty Dan has proved his unfitness to help in the great work of freeing the world from the grasp of tyranny, being him- self the greatest, as well as the most super- stitious of tyrants. From being, almost the ruler of the world, he has become the ' most effete and worn oat of nations, fit only to Iswagger around the saintly monk and the pions' ' nun. Cuba, and the Phillipine,islands are among I the fairest, and most fertile places on earth. Yet what are they at the present time ? Tne islands are in open revolt, and Cuba has for years fought for her feeedorn with a determination that commands respect. President SicKinley ice his message to I Congress says: The new and inhuman phase, happily unprecedented in the modern history of civilised Christian people, the policy of devastation and concentration, inaugurated by the Captain- General on October 21, 1896, in the province of Pinar del Rio, was thence extended to embrace all of the island to which the power of the Spanish arms was able to reach by occupation or by mili- tary operations. The peasantry,including all dwell- ing in the open agricultural interior, were driven into the garrison towns or isolated places held by the troops. The raising and movement of provi- sions of all kinds were interdicted. The fields were laid waste, dwellings unroofed and fired, mills des- troyed, and, in ehort,everything that could desolate the land and render it unfit for human habitation or support was commanded by one or other of the contending parties and executed by all the powers at their disposal. By the time the pres sent Administration took office, a year ago, recoil- centration so-called—had been made effective over the better part of the four central and western pro- vinces, Santa Clara, Matanzas. Havana and Pinar del Rio. The agricultural population, to the estimated number of 30,000 or more, was herded within the towns and their immediate vicinity, deprived of' the means of support, rendered destitute of shelter left poorly clad and exposed to the most unsatisfac- tory conditions. As the scarcity of food increased with the devastation of the depopulated areas of production,destitution and want became misery and starvation. By March, 1897, according to entser votive estimates from official Spanish sources, the mortality among the reconcentrados from starva- tion and the diseases thereto incident, exceeded 50 per cent of their total number. No practical relief was accorded to the destitute. The overburdened towns, already suffering from the general dearth, could give no aid. So-called zones of cultivation, established within the immediate area of military control about the cities and fortified camps proved illusory as a remedy for the suffering." The extract just given from the President's mes- sage is lengthy but not more than is required. The struggle may be longer and more sanguinary than some of us expect, and the Unites States Govern- ment wishes, and is anxious in this contest, which she has not by any means seught,to stand well and clear of any charges of self aggrandisement. The above quotation is not by any means sensational in its tone, nor in its trend is it exciting, it is written on conservative lines, and with the full sense of the responsibility attaching itself to exe- cutive action. The United States government has been anxious to avoid war,surely not through any pusillanimity, but as I can assure my readers because, first, from the President, Major McKinley, down to the lowest official, almost every man knows in a practical fashion what war means, and decond, there are so many features of an international character, and covered by international law which the U.S. government has been wishful to be in accord with that, to have rushed i nto war, immediately after the Maine disaster would have been criminal. I have already said that war of the most sanguinary character, and almost reaching the extermination point has been going on for years in Cuba. This led the U.S. Government to place for the defence of her citizens, and their interest a war ship. the Maine, in the harbour of Havana. Of course friendly relations existed between the two governinents,yet, on the night of the 15th of February the Maine was blown up and two hundred and fifty-eight sailors, and marines along with two officers went to their death. There can be no doubt,and there is no doubt that this disaster was brought about by Spanish treachery, although the court of enquiry, appointed by the U. S. Government, failed to locate the das- tardly wrongdoer. The President's course with res- pect to this disaster has been one, deserving of all praise. What is known here as " Yel- low Journalism, or the New Journalism" which is a natural outcome of the weak side of democratic institutions, has done its best, by sensa- tional and lying statements, to lash the people into fighting frenzy, and I have no doubt that a popular vote taken at any tine since the Maine disaster would have resulted in a demand for a declaration of war. its a matter of fact at this date of writing no declaration of war has as yet been made. I cannot pretend to go into further details of the war, as its horrid path will be followed closely by the press, and you will. like Myself, have the oppor. tunity of viewing the lurid scene each morning as you drink your coffee. The game of war is a fearful one, and not the least of its bewildering features is the feeling one always has, that unseen forces are always at work, and which the public has to wait for the future historian to lay bare. What the lady of Threadneedle-street, or the giant of Wall-street may have to do with the struggles of nations, we of the cowmen herd cannot know, " Ours net to reason why ours but to do and die," all the same we have to their struggles with foot the Bill. Nforkingmen in capital meet, nearly always, with condemnation. What shall we say of the wisdom of the struggles I between nations? What Englishman lonics backl with pride to the war with Russia? or could give, a plain statement of its why and wherefore. Barely the continued eoting of millions of pounds for war purposes is a madness without wisdom. Yet, what is known as the practical work of the world has brought America face to face with the fact that ahe cannot any longer carry out the policy of keeping herself clear of " entangling alliances." When Washington laid down this dictum as a policy he could not foresee, nor could the framers of the fa- mous American Constitution foresee the immensity of the American nation of the future. America has,' and is still more likely to have an immense corn- mercial relationship with the whole world, and she would be something more than human did she succeed in keeping clear of "entangling alliances." England has seemed during the last few months to take some pride in her "splendid isolation." She has been fain to rest satisfied with the "moral sym- pathy" of her kin beyond the sea," yet who but has felt the whole thing was but e farce. England's position in the commercial world is day by day be- coming; one of keener struggle,and it is seen that in the axiom of tht survival of the fitness, as applied to nations, the seeking of fresh woods and pastures new, has forced tbis splendid isolation upon her whether she would or no. The interests of Russia, Germany, and France are, the world over,what the interests of England are, viz : the opening up of fresh markets. It is this same anxiety on the part 110tIRDS, ANIMALS AND FISH STUFFED, AND mounted in Glass Cases any design. HEADS and HORNS mounted, also for sale. of governments to find outlets for the produce of their teaming millions that brought about the desire to pounce upon, and divide China. This dividing of China is a matter that England may well be careful about. To half a dozen nations owning the country with only artificial lann marks to keep them asunder must mean either work for the diplomat. or the keeping of immense standing armies always on guard.. _ _

Tle position taken by the after-dinner speakers, who so opportunely came to the front, was admir- able, but why the back-down? It was characteristic of English dignity to push for ward its home policy, but when once responsible statesmen had declared the ports of China should be kept open, and free to to the world it seems beyond beliet that an ignoble back-down should immediately come about. The unexpected, an I the unforeseen come upon as in politics as in our private affairs, and a short three months ago no one could have foreseen the pessible drawing together of the English speaking races, yet this is now a matter upon. which mem- bers of the House of Commons, the people of the street, the press, and I suppose not "least of all your own poetlaureate are dwelling upon with the keenest interest. America with her republican and and democratic institutions seems to stand as a menace to the monarchical institutions of Europe and it is felt they view her with no favour. The Monroe doctrine, with its hands off policy has few friends on the Earopeon Continent, England alone sees no menace in it. It fits well te the sitr ation of Canada as a British colony, and it may be that this same doctrine may be the band that shall bind mother and daughter together. If this should be AO it would mean a worlds alliance of the English speaking race, and would surely be taken as a guarantee for the world's peace and pro- gress. It alasuld do something more than this, it wmild draw the venom from the Irish vote in America and would be a fit consumriation to the work that has been going on for years in the great r religious, social and philanthropic bodies. America is not at present seeking this alliance. She is able to deal with Spain, your post laureate makes a great mistake when he quotes America as saying : What is the voice I hear

On the winds a the Western sea? Sentinel, listen. from our Cape Clear, And say what the voice may be. " Tie a Proud free People calling lon t ta people proud and free. And it says to them : "Kinsmen, hail

We svered 'nave been too Now let us have done with a worn-out tale, The tale of ancient wrong. And o r friendship last long as our love cloth, and be stronger than death is strong " It smacks somewhat of an insult to American feel- ings to make this sentinel of Cape Clear call aloud for English help in dealing with a fourth rate power like Spain. In preparing the way for an al- liance with America, English statesmen will look around for the stumbling blocks that stands in the way. They are numerous and one of the foremost is the Behring sea question. It seems to me that this apple of discord, while being very bitter to the Americans, might easily be hurled into oblivion by the British Government. Tha American Govern- ment has every argument on its side, more especi- ally does it put forward the plea of humanity in the pelagie fishing. We shall all wait for developments, the next few weeks may settle a number ot questions, and they may cause a demand for new maps, anything more serious we will not think. about. 2499,Mission-street,..7an Francisco,Calif.April 23rd, 1898.

WA.RRINGTON TEMPERANCE FEDERATION.

The annual meeting of the above Association was held in the Horsemarket-st.Cocoa Rooms on Friday evening, the chair being occupied by Councillor Ellison. There was a good attendance. Mr. W. ROBBRTS, secretary, read the report for the year ending December 31, 1897, which stated that at the last Brewster Sessions applications were made to the magistrates for increased drinking facilities in the shape of new premises and full licenses in lieu of beer licenses. As an inducement it was offered to give up certain licenses in congested districts if full licenses were granted in other parts of the town. This offer was accepted by the licensing committee in certain cases, notwithstand- ing the opposition ably conducted on behalf of a number of inhabitants. The report goes on to say this seems to be the latest move on behalf of the trade, and it behoved the magistrates to cetiously consider their responsibilities in the face of increased drinking amongst the younger portion of the community, and also amongst women. The report referred to the success of the Sunday evening lectures in the Winwick•street Co-opera- tive Hall. They rejoiced that the County magis- trates at Daresbury again refused to grant the license for a new hotel at Latchford Locks, thanks to the strong opppsition of the Ship Canal authori- ties and also to the unanimous opposition of the residents in the immediate vicinity. They are greatly pleased to record the fact that the Borough Magistrates following the example of the Justices of Liverpool and other cities and towns have unanimously agreed to instruct the Chief Constable to bring under their notice any publican known to sell intoxicating drink to children under thirteen years of age, arid also that the Watch Committee has confirmed this instruction. The needs of the Temperance movement are pressing and of urgent importance. If some of their wealthy friends would assist them to build a "Temperance. Hail," and thus provide a counter attraction to the allurements of the Public House as well as a Home for Temperance Workers, and also as a means of strengthening those who were weak in resisting the temptation of strong drink, they would greatly encourage those who through good and evil report had honestly and conscienti- ously given their best service to the cause of Tem- perance. The financial statement showed that the expendi- ture for the year had been £5O 141 8(3, and the re- ceipts .£6O lie Ild, leaving a balance in band of 1.£9 17s 3d. -

The report and statement of accounts were unanimously adopted, and the officers of the Federation were re-appointed.

A resolution was 8140 unanimously passed con- gratulating Alderman Roberts upon his elevation co the Aldermanie Bench.

The Chairman's services were acknowledged, and the meeting terminated.

On the Ilth inst., a terrible gale visited the prefecture of gwato, on the North-Bast coast of Japan. which was ravaged by the great tidal wave in 1896, Over 200 fishing boats and 1,500 rent are missing.
