Ti IL EXA ER.

bd AY 20th, 1898.

ALARMISTS are fond of di, Our food upon the risk which the nation auppiy. incurs by reason of its yearly dependence upon the produce of other lands for the food of its people, and no doubt it is conceiv- able that war might bring this island within measurable distance of the straits to which Paris was brought in the Franco-German war—assum- ing., of course, that our navy had its Sedan. But, without joining the ignoble army of croakers, it is worthy of con.ilaration what our position is in regard ,to the food supply from abroad. The fact that our daily bread is for the greater part of the year supplied from across the ocean is pretty well understood. As a matter of fact, our own wheat crop, on an average, will feed the population of these islands for about four months out of the twelve. Whereas now the quantity of wheat and flour imported amounts to two hundred and fifty , pounds per head of population, twenty years ago it amounted to only one hunched and sixty- three pounds per head, and thirty years ago to one hundred and twenty-seven pounds. It is noteworthy, also, how largely of late years the quantity of foreign fleur has in- creased ; the increase amounting in the course of ten years to nearly fifty per cent. This, it is scarcely necessary to say, is regrettable, because, while it makes no appreciable difference to the consumer, to whom it is the same whether wheat is imported in a ground or unground condition, yet the large importation of flour instead of wheat affects disastrously the milling industry of the country, and as a secondary result raises the price of the " bye-products " such as bran, sharps, pollards, and other " offals." The countries whence our chief supplies of breadstuffs come are the United States, which sends us about one-third of the total wheat and three-fourths of the total flour; India, which sends about one- fifth of the wheat; Russia, Canada, Chili, the Argentine Republic, and Australia. During the first four months of the present year the United States supplied England with 12,280,300 cwts of wheat; Russia, 3,371,400Twt5.; and the Agentine Republic with a little more than two million cwts. The importation of meat has ,prro- gressively increased by leaps and bounds in recent years, and in the four months designated the United States sent us no fewer than 149,393 liv- ing cattle for food ,whilstvast quantities of cbil led mutton came from Australia and Argentina. The total trade in dead meat in those months reached the large total af 5)290,070 cwts., and .is valued at more than nine and a half millions sterling. The losses of stock caused by outbreaks of disease in- troduced from abroad are disastrous both to the farmers whose stock is attacked,and to consumers., generally. By reducing the home stock, outbreaks of contagious disease have done more than any- thing else to raise the price of meat. It is ,very, difficult to estimate what relation the amount of foreign dairy produce consumed in this country bears to the home produce. So far as estimates are available it appears that about three-fifths of. the butter and one-half the cheese consumed in Great Britain are of foreign origin. It is a fact which is not regarded generally as being creditable to the farmers of this country. No doubt they. are greatly handicapped, not only by unfair com- petition, as in the case of margarine, and adulter-• ated butter, but still more seriously by the bounty, given to foreign produce by some of the English, railway companies, who seem to strain every point to foster the trade in foreign produce, while doing very little indeed to help the home producer, The question of adulteration crops up now and again, and the present Government is under a pledge to deal with it in a practical manner, while that of railway rates and freightage, about which the feeling in the country has been so bitter is also, receiving more consideration in some parts of the country than it ewer did before. With regard totbe pre- sent price of bread, there is no reason why it should be affected by the war between the United • States and Spain in the way we are asked to be- hove it has been. Corn is not contraband, and there is nothing to account for an nun-ease of fifty per cent, in the price of a loaf beyond what is purely speculative on the part of the merchant, Agriculture seldom profits by a sudden rise in price such as we have seen within the past fort- night or so. The merchant sets the ball rolling, the miller and the baker pass it on, and the con- sumer pays and grumbles as long as the game lasts, When it is over,the remedies he has been puzzling his brains about are forgotten, anti may twt tie

_ _

. •

given auether thought until tl.e priee tins up Mal more. Happily the price of bread does not bring with it fear of hunger and starvation wucLi such an increase as has now been experienced would have created not so many years ago. Fortunately this year the people are able to bear the extra cost of a necessity of life better than in previous years, and only the poorest suffer more than usual is consequence of dearnwe of bread. Taking the community as a whole, the country has prospered so immensely since the dawn of Free Trade that a penny on the four-pound loaf carries with it comparatively little distress. Men are in a better ' position ; their incomes are higher ; and the cost of living is less. Men not far past the middle age will remember when bread was the chief article of food, and other foods which are now regarded as 'necessaries were simply luxuries and rare luxuries too. "Man does not live by bread alone," though it is still one of the essentials of a nourishing diet. It is the predominant, but not the sole food in the sense in which it was regarded prior to the Victorian era.

It is agreeable to remember The Grand Old Man. that to within a few months bince, Mr. GLADSTON.S—nOW, alas, no more—still retained the power of deliver- ing those charming autobiographical speeches with which in the past few years he delighted so , many audiences. Every speech of this kind:by " the man of the century" was to be valued ; for there could not be many more of them, andtit, was touching and beautiful to find his non- political addresses so marked by serenity, cheer-- fulness, and playfulness. These characteristics. were signs of the bright and happy evening that followed a well-spent working day, and it was a. lesson of no slight value to persons who are in- clined to narrowness of view to find Mr. GLADSTONE, bordezing upon his ninetieth year; gratefully reviewing the progress which had been made, and eagerly anticipating the happy lot of future generations. We know that there are always persons who are tho admirers of other times in preference to their own. It was so in the days of Eloaacm, and will be so centuries hence. But the .majority of men are not so dis- contented. They are glad to compare their posi- tion favourably with that of other people, and it may be just as well for our iwn peace of mind that we do not know what our successors will have to boast of. Our ignorance of the future is at least one matter which we share with our ancestors, upon whom we look back with a lofty sense of superiority. Mr. GLADSTONE less than twelve months since assured us that it was a clever man who prophesied that railways would promote highway robberies, his argument being that as the rich people would not use them, and the poor would, the former would have the highways to themselves, and would* thus give opportunities to highwaymen. If it had not been for Mr. GransrorrE's voucher for the clever- ness of this prophet, the natural tendency would be to quote the saying as an instance of the fatuity and ignorance of people who had the mis- fortune to live in pr,,-scientific days. There is no mental dissipation more common than that of fancying what these predecessors of ours, who foretold disasters to trains,and said that a steam- ship could never cross t'f.e A dantic,would think if they could be re-called to life and shown an ex- press and an ocean liner, and have the wonders of , the telegraph and the telephone explained to them, although we who thus exult over our pre- decessors are absolutely innocent of any share in the great progress of which we boast. In a collec- tive sense we may, perhaps,be justified in compar- ing the present with the past, with a sons, of the immense advantages to accrue to " the heirs of all the ages." In his boyhood, said Mr. GL STONE once, "the whole of what had to Vut between the vast city of Liverpool and tendon every day was comprised within the limits of a single mail coach." The statement revealed as bY a flash the enormous changes that- have been made by railways, aided by the ()thew devices for the de- velopment of commerce,and'established the truth thatin no century since history began has there been anything like the social, commercial, and in- dustrial revolution within the period of a hundred years that has occurred in this,land in the century that is now drawing to a close_ Men whose lives have been so protracted as that of Mr: GLAD' STONE have lived in a transition age; and it would be the rankest folly,the most culpable blindness,to pretend that all is well with, us now,that there are no social evils to be overcome,uo pitiable wrong to be righted.

From all quarters support is given to the pro- ject for compelling vehicles- to 'tarry lights after, dark, in the same manner as cyclists are thus, effected by the law. In Uheshire,as is well-known locally, lighting of all classes of vehicles is cow-, pulsory, and a great boon it is to cyclists as well as pedestrians. Even tluzi powerful voice of the "Lancet "—whose connection with the subject may be rather obscure—is raised in support of universal lighting. This influential journal urges that the regulation should ho made to ' apply to all vehicles in every part of the country. - It is quite absurd in this respect to attempt to.' classify vehicles using.. the public roads ; all with-t- -out exception should be compelled to carry visible signals which will poiut the proper course to steer and thus ensure the safety of the publia' both on foot and on wheels. The danger, as cur contemporary points out, is no imaginary one. -With the greatest care a cyclist may be proceed- - ing at night down-hill, aware that there is scnae- thing ahead, but by no means sure in what direction it is going, with the result that he mai suddenly find himself in contact with a paover-r ful horse, or the back of a vehicle. Should he escape serious injury the cyclist gots a fright and shock which will effectively deprive him of any pleasure in riding, especially, after dusk, for some time to come. Is there ie. no eh— jection of a serious character raised to the.pro- posed compulsory lighting it is a matter.-for sur— prise that so many authorities have failed to put' such a beneficent law le force long ago.

Margarine is sometimes a dangerous com- modity to its vendors, as was instanced at the "Pense of a Battersea grocer. The tradesman Waa summoned for selling as butter a substance ?entaining 70 per cent of margarine, and his de- fence was that he was protected from criminal pro- Feedings by the fact that the substance was served a wrapper marked with the word" Margarine." ne name was placed in a corner of the paper, °lit the solicitor for the defence said this Was immaterial so long as the name was on the wrapper. The magistrates decided against this 3: rather elastic interpretation of the Act, flnd in fining the deli,aquent £3 and costs said the name should be put where it could be seen. A Instomer was not expected t hunt for it. We unvel had one or two prosecutions not unlike Warrington,though not precisely the same, and all of them go to show that too much care cannot be exercised in the sale of margarine if the vendor is to keep without the expensive clutches Of the law.

In connection with the annual trip there has been some diversity of opinion as to the destination which will secure a good day's outing. At first Shrewsbury was fixed upon, but owing to the great distance and the amount of time spent in travelling back and to, it has now been decided to forego Shrewsbury, and on Monday night,after some discussion it was unanimously decided to spend the day at Llangollen. Whit-Tuesday is the day fixed for the out.

It was further decided to have a concert, entirely of local talent, in the Town Hell, on Monday next (May 23rd), when an effort will be made to secure the services of a speaker who will be able to give a description of the distress in the affected district.

THE EX 'MINER, FRIPAY, MAY 20 1898.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[By A. CORTIESPONDBNT.I There are just a few things in Runcorn which could do with a little " improvement." Of course, there are! Everybody knows that ! But how to secure that "little improvement " is the question that agitates the majority of Runcornians. Some people would bring us face to face with our water supply. They would ferce home to us the fact that the water supply of Runcorn is bad ; aye, Very bad. But surely, if it is the desire of Runcorn to improve things all round, it would be better to pursue a course of investigation and see whether the water supply is what it might be. It it were excellent, then there could be no room for improve- ment : but if it did prove defective, see what a great deal might be gained. A thorough investiga- tion by competent engineers, in the nature of a general over-hauling ef things, might be expensive; but asudden outbreak of an epidemic of any serious nature would have a more disastrous effect than the greatest folly in the nature of a false economy, so called, could ever possibly bring about.

The grave has closed -over the mortal remains of the young man Hassell, who met his death at the Highfield Tannery last week. The details of the acci- dent bring before us the great and growing need there is for a hospital of some kind in Runcorn. That a sufferer should, in addition to the injuries sustained in any accident, have to undergo a long and tedious journey by rail to Liverpool berate he can obtain the necessary medical and surgical skill and appliances to cope with those injuries, brings this need before us :with irresistible force. Tots does not imply that in the case quoted above, the patient did not receive what attention could be be- stowed upon him by local practitioners—far from, it; for we know that all that medical skill could do was brought to bear upon the case. But, the writer's object is to press home to the hearts of our " city fathers" the great need of an accident or cottage hospital in Runcorn, where in the case of accident the local practioner could find close to hand every apdliance and convenience to aid him in alleviating the sufferings of any unfortunate person whose con- dition necessitated the extreme course of amputa- tion,

Especially so, in a district brim full of large works where people bring their lives into jeopardy. in the earning of their daily bread, is there need 'for such an accident hospital, and the wonder is that some of those ardent spirits who go abroad vaunt- ing about their doings, seeing " moun- tains" where other mortals only see " mole-hills " have not exerted themselves in this direction. Yet, perhaps, it is well that the necessity should first be felt for an object of this nature to obtain a eym- p,.thic reception, only let U 3 hope something may be done before it is too late.

The committee for the relief of the distress in the West of Ireland is putting forward its work with a view of obtaining a little tangible relief for our fellew-creatures in the West of Ireland. At the meeting on Friday last,a step in the right direction was taken when collectors were appointed to can- vas from door to door. But there was a great deal in the remarks to which Mr.Lightburn gave utterance, when he said thatthere was considerable misconcep- tion with regard to the poverty and want,the abject Pitiable condition of things in the West of Ireland. There required a great effort to keep the case of these distressed mortals before the public, as the true facts were lost sight of in the craze for news from the Far East,and the American-Spanish War. If they intended to be of any material assistance they must keep the public well informed so as to elicit their sympathies in so worthy a cause. Much could be gained by a house to house canvass of the district, hut unless ~he object was well understoed there would be very little response, and much valu- able time lost in explaining the state of affairs. That there is distress in the West of Ireland, and distress of a nature which baffles all description is only too true. For instance, take the case where half-a-dezen people varying from th s ages of 45 to 7 years, herd together in a portion of a room 12 feet by 10 feet, and in the other portion there is a cow or pig, or perchance, as in many other cases a litter of pigs. Surely such a state of things is enough to gain our sympathies, our help, and to cause us to exert our energies on their behalf. There may be nearer claims for l)u r consideration, but there never was a greater need of help than in the ease of the poor— chronic poor—of the West of Ireland.

BEFORE THE M&GISTRATES.

ME BENCH AND THE CHIRP CONITABLE.

On Monday befori. Mr. S. Wright (in the chair), Coun- t illor James Smethurst, Councillor Shaw-Green, and Alderman Blackhurst,Thom as Mulkearns, labourer, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Platt- street on the 9th May.—This case arose out of the recent diaturhance in Platteitreetwhen a woman was seriously Injured by another woman with a poker, her assailant being committed to prison fora month. From the evi- dence of P.C. Lewis,it appeared that the defendant Mul- kearns acted in a very violent manner,and threatened to make use of a knife he had in his hand.—P.C. Corbett said he heard the defendant threaten to stab the Con- etables with the knife if they came into his house after him. A large number of witnesses were called on behalf of the defendant. —The Chief Constable said the police officers were badly treated on the night in question by the crowd. It was this class of people who bee the police in the streets, and out-swore them in the court. That was the position of affairs.—The Bench were pro- Seeding to discuss the matter prior to giving their deci- sion, when the Chief Constable interposed, and said that if the preponderance of evidence of the class he had referred to was to be taken as against that of the police, their occupation, like that of Othello's would be gone.—Couneillor Smethurst: We have a right to take evidence for the public as well as for the police. —The Chief Constable : Quite so.—Councillor Smeth- urst : There are three witnesses who spoke distinctly as to what took place, and I don't think you have a right to make such remarks in the Court.—The Chief Con- stable : I have a perfect right, as prosecutor, as your Clerk will tell you, to put the case before the Ce urt to the best of my ability, and with respect to everybody.— Councillor Smethurst : We know that. —Councillor Shaw-Green : My opinion is that the Chief Constable is most careful in weighing the evidence.—Alderman Slackhurst : Still we cannot ignore the weight of evidence.—The Chief Constable : I simply point out that this is the evidence of friends and neighbours. I would not at all say that the police are infallible, and that their evidence should always be taken as truth.—The Chair- man : The decision ot the Bench is that the defendant be lined Sc. and costs.

THEFT/ FROM WARRINGTON MARKET. On Thursday before Mr. Geddes (in the chair), Mr. ames Hepherd, Alderman James Pairclough and Alder- Thomas Sutton. Jane Mather was charged with steal- ing a pair cf stockings value Is. ld., and a girl's bonnet, value 2s. Id. from two different stalls.—rho woman pleaded guilty.—Detective Inspector Monaghan's evi- dence was to the effect that about 4 o'clock on Wednes- day he watched the woman Mather as her movements were suspicious. He saw her take a pair of stockings from the stall of Mr. Williams Mullins, and a girl's bonnet from the stall of Mr. Thomas Edward Leigh. He followed the woman up and she endeavoured to evade him by going into an entry to get rid of the article,. He took her in charge and pointed to the stolen goods under her cloak. On taking her down to the Police Station, she threw the articles into a shop. He saw the ant and secured the articles. The woman had been up four times before for drunkenness.—Alderman Sutton remarked there was so much temptation in the market it was no wonder people pilfered.—The prisoner was sent to gaol for 21 days. NEGLECT 07 CHILDREN AT Wanninciroar. At the Warrington Police Court, on Thursday. before Mr. Geddes (in the chair), Mr. James Hepherd, Alder- men James Fairclough, and Alderman Thomas Sutton, Patrick Macdonald and Mary Mannion were charged with neglecting two children. One was the 7 years old child of Mary Mannion (who is a single woman) and the other was the child of Macdonald, named Mary, eight years of age. The man and woman lived together at 7, Walsh's-yard, Old-road. Both pleaded guilty.—lnspec- tor McAteer,who prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C. said on the night of the 17th inst., in consequence of a complaint received he and P.C. Clough went to the house. They found the two girls, one !aged seven and the other eight years sitting before the grate. The house was in a most filthy condition and very scantily fur- nished. The children were well nourished, but in a most neglected condition. He went upstairs where the man Macdonald and the woman Mannion were. They were in a dirty condition, and ,the woman half naked, Elbe made use of disgraceful language to him. The man however was very quiet.—P.C. Clough corroborated the Inspector's evidence—The defendants expressed their regret and said they would endeavour to do better in future.—The Chief Constable said this was the woman's 12th appearance and the man's 21st appearance.—The magistrates sent the woman Mary Manakin to prison for one month with hard labour, and the ruin Macdonald for fourteen days hard labour.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

ROUSH OF LORDS.-MONDAY.

Trt the House of Lords on Monday the Merchant Shipping (Liability of Shipowners) Bill was read a second time. on the motion of the Marquis of LOvDoNDERRY. Viscount SID- HoiTra asked whether, in view of Natal's offer to supply coal for the use of the navy, it was intended to take any steps to render tne harbour of Durban readily accessible to Her Majesty's ships. Lord Hointrous, in r-piy, said the harbour I had already been made accessible to ships drawing no more than 17 feet. but as the bar was a shifting one it would be in- advisable to spend large sums of money on increasing the draught over the bit:. The Government, therefore, did not think lt advisable to convert Port Durban into an Imperial coaling station. HOUSE OF COMMONS.—MONDaY. In the House of Commons on Monday Mr. ARCROFT asked whether the statement attribute i to Count Muravieff, that it was England alone that made difficulties and stood in the way of Russia, and that n other Government had ques- tioned him officially on the subject or seemed to resent in any degree the pr: posed action of Russia in China, was correct. Mr. OURZoR replied that the Government were, of course, not in a position to know what csnamunications had or had not passed between the Russian and other Governments.—Asked by Mr. Heiderick if French troops were still in occupation of posts within the area of the British West African Protectorate declared and announced to France on January 1, 1895, Mr Curson said he must remind the hon. member that he had more than once stated that while negotiations were pending he could not undertake to make any statement upon the matters in dispute.—Mr. Yerburgh asked whether it could be arranget that the Foreign Office vote •hould. be taken on the first Friday after the Whitsuntide r .cess. —Mr. BALFOUR replied that it was neither easy nor expedient to I lallocate pa, ticular Fridays much more than a week before- hand. He understood that Mr. Yerburgh and Sir C. Dilke had questions on the same subject, tmt, they were not in agreement with the topics they wished to discuss. Mr. Terburgh desired to deal with the policy of the Government lin China, while Sir C Mike desired to consider their general foreign poiicsy.—Sir W. Harcourt said what be Ilthought the House and the country would desire to discuss was the Birmingham foreign policy, Mr. A. W. Seamel, the new Liberal member for South Norfolk, took the oath and I his seat in succession to Mr. F. Taylor, resigned. The House afterwards went into Committee on the Local Government (Ire:end) Bill, Clause 33 was passed without further discussion. On clause 34, which relates to the agri- cultural grant. Mr. STRACHEY proposed that the grant should be limited to a period of five years. —Mr. G. BILYoUR said that when the Government proposed to allot £l5O GOO to Ireland as a set-off against the money given to England by the Act of 1396 they were proposing an equivalent grant. What they were now proposing was identity of treatment. As the agricultural grant and the new system of local govern- ment essentially held together, they could net upset one. without upsetting the other, and the Government were not prepared to make a revolution in Ireland to be reconsidered in five years.—Two Conservative members,Mr. G. WHITELEY and Mr. Csisszu.s, supported the amendment, which was however, rejected by 258 votes to 157. On the motion that the clause stand part of the Bill a division was taken, aud the motion was carried hy 243 votes to 63. HOUSE OF LORDS,—TUESDA.Y. In the House of Lords on Tuesday. Lord Kt vs MILEY asked the Government what were their intentions with regard to the occupation of Wel-Hal-Wei. He discussed, in doing so. the Far Eastern policy of the Government, with special reference to Mr. Chamberlain's speech. Unless some con- siderable strategical advantage was to be gained, the occupa• Den of this port would be, he said, a great burden. With reference to Mr. Chamberlain's allusion to the question of a European alliance on the part of this country, he asked 'whether the moment had arrived when so great a change from our p 0 icy was to brought into effect, and he also asked I himself- could it be that the Government was so far advanced lin that direction that ere long we might hear of the contain- /ton of some great alliance with a powerful European nation The time might not be far distant when an actual alliance might be concluded, and no possible step could be taken more ikely to prevent the conclusion of such an alliance than to make such statements as he had made, audit was from that ' point of view that he complained bitterly of such statements being made.—Lord SALIsI3-MT. in reply. declined to enter Into any discussion upon bin Chamberlain's speech, as he had not isad notice, and had not a copy of the speech with him. One of the defeuces of the occupation of Wei-Hal-Wei, he went on to say, was that it had a tendency to strengthen China against despair, and give her courage, if the occasion ever arose, to stand up against danger. There was a survey Made some years ago by the Admiralty, from which It would be clearly seen that the port was a very valuable station. It was situated in the midst of a region rapidly growing in importanee. and commercially likely to increase, which could not be served by a station so far away as Hong Kong. As to the Government's general policy, they wished to see the Chinese Empire maintained and its government reformed, and the building up of a splendid industrial and commercial structure.

HOUSE OF COMMONS.—TuEsnAy. In the House of Commons on Tuesday Mr. LI /LLCM asked If the British Commandant at Candle had allowed the Turkh Is regular troops to advanoe and occupy positions hitherto outside the cordon whether the Bashi-Bazauks were allowed to pass freely through the Turkish lines, whether reeently they cut down the olives beloaging to a Christian village ne Sr the British post of Anopolls, whether the servant of an anglish traveller was recently arrested by the Turks in Omni and. thrown Into a loathsome dungeon, and whether Colonel Chermaide, on being appealed to. refused to interfere, saying that he was there to co-operate with the Turks. Lord G. HAMILTON stated that the allegation contained In the question had not been brought to the notice of the Govern' meat. An arrangement had recently been made with the consent of both parties for the extension of the cordon outside Candi& and for the occupation of salient points by British troops In advance of the Turkish regulars, the Bashi- Batbuks being withdrawn to Candi& Colonel Oheemside had, however. informed the Christians that if this experiment did not succeed he would revert to the former systera. —ln reply to Mr. J. B. Bilis, Sir M. Elicxa-BNICH said £798.802 had been al,eanced to the Egyptian Treasury, and was still owing on the 31st of March last. The Egyptian Government agreed to pay 'rf per cent interest, but none had yet been paid. As to the futnre, he expected to have to make a proposal to the House on the subject. Mr. ASOttoFT asked whether the Government intended to make any statement in respect to the speech made by Mr. Chamber- lain last Friday. Mr. BALFOUR replied that they did not. The House af t-rwards went into Committee on the Lace Government (Ireland) Bill. Clauses 35 and 38 were, after a brief discussion, adde ito the bill. Oa clause 37, which pro- vides that the poor rate shall be made upon the occupier and not the landlord. Mr. Dxtree moved that the proposal of the bill levy- ing a poor rate on the occupier alone should not apply in may city or borough. The amendment was, after a brief discus- sion, withdrawn. Clause 40 load been added to the bill when the hour for the adjournment of the debate arrived.

SOCIAL P 110BLEMS.

THE LEND FOR, THE PEOPLE,

FINAL ARTICLE. By JOSEPH HYDER, GENERAL SECRETARY to the

Land Nationalisation Society.

ARTICLE XXIII.

The Land Question means hunger, thirst, nakedness, notice to quit; labour spent in vain, the toil of years seized up-in, the breaking up of homes ; the misery, sickness, and death of parents, children, and wives; the despair and wild- ness which spring up in the hearts of the poor, when legal force, like a sharp harrow, goes over the most sensitive and vital rights of mankind. All this h contained in the land question.---CARDINAL MANNING. Irs this series of articles, which now conclude, I have endeavoured to show that the question of the land lies at the root of the social problem, and that that problem can nsver be solved apart from the:fearless and thorough grappling with the land difficulty. I commenced with an appeal to the trade unionists to take practical steps in f adherence of the principle of land nationalisation, which the Trade Union Congresses have again and again supported. I traced the comparative helplessness of labour to its main cause—the landless condition of the workers. I showed tbat the labour market is always more or less glutted, chiefly as the result of the depopu- lation of the country dietricts, and I contended that no permanent and adequate improvement in the condition of the people in possible unless something be done to check that depopulation. I pointed to the steady growth of the big towns,and to the:deterioration in the physique of the main body of our countrymen,which is inevitable when the fresh air and free space of the country give place to the unhealthy coneritioneof our crowded centres. The question of the housing of the poor, was else dealt with, and I essayed to prove that the general raising of the standard of comfort in and around the home cannot be secured while it is in the interests of private indi- viduals to make the most of their land by crowding houses upon it in utter regardiessnese of the minimum of space that is necessary for the development of healthy human lives • I also referred to the confiscation by land- lords both of the improvements made by tenant s ended the values caused by the growth of the community. The question a the heavy tribute with which the British mining industry is handicapped was dealt with, together with the problems of the unemployed, and of our dan- gerous dependence upon other countries for our food supplies ; and, finally, I think I have established the fact that the State has a perfect right, on equitable terms, to acquire the freehold of the land which is now vested in individuals. one object is to unite reformers, and to SOsfsfON PLATFORM FOR reeeeßmints In all this ray concentrate their efforts upon the deetruction of the land monopoly, which Constitutes the main barrier to the disunion progress lasts, na- tion's real prosperity. It is a grief to me to see the dividedr will be eaction against each o master of the situation, and monopoly and privilege will forces til r . which i longeh m a as e f thise r reign unchecked. Differences of opinion there must be. Without them no forward step was ever taken. But why seould not an arrangement be arrived at between Lib- erals and Radicals on the one hand and Socialists on the other hand? Surely they have some points et faith in common. Do they not all agree that the obstructive newer of that nineteenth-century anomaly the House of Lord. should be effectually broken? Do they not all agree that the franchise should be more widely extended, that education should be more widely diffused, and that justice should be more impar- tially administered for poor and rich alike ? Moreover,the gene lesson is being gradually forced upon the minds of all who are at all imbued with the re- forming spirit that some better way must be found for the equitable apportionment of the nation's wealth among those who actually create it. And I cherish the hope that ere long we may find reformers agreed in de- fining the limits of private property. Already we see that local communities in increasing numbers recognise , the superiority of public ownership over private owner- ship when tram ways and the supply of gas and water are concerned. A growing volume of public opinion declares for the public ownership and management of the railway monopoly. No one would dream of taking the gigantic letter carrying and telegraph businems from the Post Office, which has managed it so well. And so, in this and other directions, it is clear that the general tendency of modern times is for the state to acquire monopolies. It is simply in harmony with that tendency that the State should acquire the greatest monopoly of all, that of theland. "AN OPPOSITION SHOP." Let us suppose that a thorough step has been taken towards that object by the liberal extension of powers of compulsory land purchase to local authorities, and that the voters ate wise enough to elect democratic repre- sentatives. Let us suppose that the Town Council gra- dually takes the place of the ground landlord, and the parish or District Council that of the squire. A very important result would accrue from even such a partial establishment of public landownership. Competition be- tween the public and private owners would be insti- tuted. Each would compete for tenants,and would have to offer good terms to get them. The public landlords would give security of tenure,at a fair and periodically- revisable rent. Is it not clear that the private landlords would be compelled to follow suit ? Indeed, the effect of such campetition in providing the terms of contracts made by occupiers of land can scarcely be oves-estim- ated. At present the landlords have matters all their own way. They play into each other's hands, sad they are little subject to competition. But the proposed change would be equivalent to the opening of an " op- position shop " for the supply of an article (land) in which they now have the tnynopoly. And I anticipate the very greatest benefit therefrom as a moans of break- ing their oppressive power. It would certainly lower the artificially created value of the laud they hold, and , would make its acquisition by the State much more easy to accomplish,

===== 0002601_18980520_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
EFFECT ON WAGES:

But, besides operating in favour of all tenants as such, it would immensely benefit wage-earners as well. For it would open out far them an alternative to wage. service, which, even although not many might avail themselves of it, would relieve the labour market, and thus tend both to the f aising of wages and to the reduc- tion of the hours of lobour. lam supposing that any man could go to the Communal authority and usingnddem(nanodt his birthright; the right of occupying an owning) on fair terms a share of the soil of his native land. In villages these are always some of the best men who could avail themselves of this opportunity. And thus, little by little, a new set of occupiers would be created who would be more or lees independent of hired employment. And this weeding-out process would make employment available for many who now fail to And work of any regular kind. Thus the unemployed would be gradually absorbed. And as the system extended we should see that even many of the workers from the towns would realise the advantages of a eountry life and a healthful occupation in the open air. So that wages would tend to rise in proportion as labour, having two strings to its bow, becdme more and more independent. Wages would rise to the level ef what a man could get by working for himself, either as a separate individual or as a member of a co-operative undertaking. Every- one knows that the proximity of a coal mine to a farm raises the wages of the farm labourer towards the level of the wages of the miner. In like manner the wide prevalence of small holdings, easily obtainable, would beneficially affect the wages of ordinary hired labourers. No man would work for another for less than he could get by working for himself. FAIR WAGS FOE FAIR WORK. And this is what I want to see. I want a man's re- ceipts on Saturday night to approximate as usarly as may be to his actual earnings. "A fair day's wage for a fair day's work." No more and no less. Surely nut an unreasonable demand. But it is not to be got, as some apparently imagine, by denouncing capitalists as the incarnation of evil. Wages are not determined by the disposition or character of employers. The price of labour, like the price of any other thing is simply deter- mined by supply and demand. Therefore the scientific and in fact the only sure way of improving the condi- tion of labour is by relieving the pressure in the labour market. And such relief can be found by the opening up of the land. THE CI7LTIYATOR OF THE FIITURS. No man can yet foresee what the productive capacity of land really is when cultivated with industry and technical knowledge. I believe that the time has come for the adoption of a more intensive system of cultiva- tion than has yet been the rule in our country. The old system of farming will do more for the future. The farmer of the future will bea man of science. lie will know as much of chemistry as the farmer of to-day knows of horses. He will plough a good deal deeper than the usual five inches, for he will know that the aeration of the subsoil is as necessary as the putting in of the seed. Be will have a good deal more than the £5 per acre capital to which the majority of tiritish farmers have nowadays been reduced. He must not have a holding greater than he can properly work. He will have the assurance that the improvements he effects will be his own property, and will not be confis- cated by an outsidc idler. He will, I hope, learn the ad- vantages of co-operative industry in certain departments. He will be better cnabled to fight foreign competitors as , the full effects of land nationalisation begin to be felt, ' and the Government gets its revenue from the rent of land ihstead of from the taxation of the product of labour. Be must learn to get his produce into the hands of the censurner with THE MINIMUM OF EXPENSE.

===== 0002601_18980520_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY MAY 20, 1898.

And I venture to predict he will be aided, before many years have gone by. by the public control of the main railway system, and by the establishment of a network of Ugh!: railways throughout the country districts, hav- ing for their first object the serving of the general com- munity rather than the exclusive interests of share- holders. And thus the great national estate will be de- veloped,the most will be made of its resources,the whole field of natural opportunities will be open to all, a vast home trade will be created, and a comfm table livinf, will be within the reach of all who honestly deserve it oy useful labour. Doubtless some will regard all this as a I Utopia. It has been the late of every salutary reform of the past to be so regarded. Yet the Utopia ot one day becomes the reality, of the next, and what were but dreams in our grandfather's time are cunmonplaces In our own. 8o it will be with the land question. It will loom larger and larger in the public mind. Its import- ance will be gradually apprehended by the majorityof the people. The need for a chance will become more ' and more apparent, the work of education will go on, ' and the first stages of a new land system will be brouht in almost before men realise what the logical outcome will be.

===== 0002601_18980520_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
CONCLUSION.

Thus will be inaugurated, peaceably and equitably, a reform that will become a beneficial revolution. The people will enter their long alienated inheritance. They will cease to be the prey of a privileged class who live by the exaction of a tribute for the use of a gift of nature. And when undeserved wealth vanishes, undeserved poverty will quickly become a thing of the past.

It's coming yet are long. When the right shall ding the wrong. And our rulers learn that cattle are of less account than men.

And the struggle shall be won, And justice shall be done. And the people b hall enjoy their own again I

FOR ALL BILIOUS AND NERVOUS DISORDERS,

SUCH AS

Sick Headache, Constipation,

C RICKET.

Pi arrington took advantage of the open date on Satur- day to test the abilities of the junior cricketers. Thewlis assisted the kiniors. With. a wet wicket the bowling bad quick reaults. Crompton's score of 12 was the best of the sixteen. Bibby's bowling was of a very searching character, 23 runs only beiag scored against him for eleven wickets, a very fine performance indeed ! Of the 'first eleven,Bibby who carried his bat out for 27 and Con- nor's 28 were the best batting totals. The game ended in a win for the firs.c. eleven. St. Jaines'.4 on tfaeir own ground on Saturday gave Widnes Temperance a decisive heating. Edison, Darling- ton, Gorst (not Oat) and White proved themselves the 'best bats of the homestere on this occasion. St. James score reached the respectable figure of 101 against 42. The visitors did not do much in the way of batting. Young's 12 topped the list.

It is understoAd that excelsior means the best, and the Arpley Excelsiors proved themselves better than St. Barnabas at Lovgly-lane. A win is a win if it is only by six.

-- Sankey Wire Mills visited Birkenhead Grosvenor on Saturday—for a defeat as it turned out. Lea by neat batting compiled a nice 28 for the homesters. R. Cook and Simpsonicr the wire-men made the best score.

S. Albans when they visited Latchford White Star were traten by 33 runs. H. 'Gorton and Ford compiled most for the White Star. Ford's four wickets for three runs was gond. There was no individual batting amongst St. Albans, whose score was 31 against 88 for White Star.

CYCLING.

On Saturday last about 35 members of the Warring- ton Cycle Club turned out for the run to Hale via Wool. ton and Gateacre. The weather was good and the road in excellent condition. After partaking of tea, the ramble down to the shore and lighthouse was greatly enjoyed.

Apropos of my remarks last week anent loose bottom t ackets in many cases too,the looseness is owing to the cup or cups being slightly smaller than the barrel of the bracket, with the result that even when the split lug is screwed up until the ends met, the cup is still loose, and could be moved with a little extra pressure of the peg. wrench. Of course, such a bearing would unscrew with the running friction of the balk. Any of my readers who are troubled with this particular bearing constantly working loose should carefully examine tha lag to ascertain whether the ends touch ; if so you can- not get the barrel any tighter, and will require a new cup of slightly bigger diameter.

Several speed motor cycles, principally of the single tricycle type, have lately been seen along the Brighton road, and have afforded a good opportunity for a bit of free pace to several "bangers on." Many tall tales of their moving are being tald, and that the speed brigade appreciate them is evident. A rather novel sight seen last Sunday was that of a motor tricycle, evidently broken down, being towed along by a tandem pair. The tricycle rider was trying to work the supplementary chain gear, hut looked as though he could have made but little progress without the towing assistance.

It has now been decided that the " Century "Cup is to be raced for by professionals, in which Cordang, Wal- ters, Palmer, and Armstrong, will take part. The first race will be run on Whit Monday, when Cordang and Walters will race over 100 miles. The second will be run between Palmer grid Armstrong, and the final will be run off between the winners of the two, and we hear on very good authority that the fortunate winner will, in addition, have the honour of being the big tyro company's repmsantative in the 100 kilometers world's championship. If the pacing can only be fairly distri- buted, the races should afford grand contests.

There are again this y .r scores of new, and many of them inexperienced, cycle-makers flooding the market with their wares. Yet, though thero is so much compe- tition, it is pleasant to find most of the older firms keeping their heads above water without much difficulty. Merit will always find a place, and it is this alone which has found the New Bowe Machine Company, , Limited, Glasgow, such a prominent position in the cycling world. New Bowe cycles scarcely need any recommendation, but I may just state here that the road-racer made by this firm, which I have ridden for nearly twelve months, has given every satisfaction. It is undoubtedly the smoothest-running, and best hill- climbing cycle I have ever yet bestrode. Mr. J. S. Gar- land, Warrington, is the local agent for New Howe cycles.

CYCLING.

According to latest news from America,the three Lon- don cyclists, who are riding round the world for "Travel," are now away in the alkali desert of Nevada, having just completed their sixteen thousand miles of riding. Till the Mississippi River is reached, the trio, Messrs. Kisser, Lunn, and Lowe, are following the rail- way track,and before leaving California,they rode over the Sierra Nevada range of mountains, passing through fotty miles of snow sheds. The thousand mile, over Nevada desert is the last piece of difficult travelling the adventurers have before getting back to England, after an absence of two years. Though this dc-ert is the terror of America, and the Cyclists suffered from want of water, they declare their troubles were as nothing' compared with what they had experienced in China, Curma, and Persia.

WARRINGTON.

IRISH NATIoNAL LEAGUL—WARIZINGTON BRANCH. On Sunday afternoon in the Druids HalLSankey- street,a meeting of this branch of the Irish National League, was held, and the President took the chair. A resolution of sympathy with Mr. Gladstone in his sad affliction was unanimously adopted. The Presi- dent read "The Crimson Stain," and gave an address on the agrarian question. Messrs. Noon and Lane, the proposer and seconder of the resolution above mentioned, addressed the meeting in terms of the greatest sympathy with Mr. Gladstone,whose advo- cacy of Home Rule for Ireland will, they said, ever be remembdred by the Irish race with feelings of gratitude.

At this hall oneunday afternoon Mrs. Newton,ef Stockport, delivered an address on "If a man die shall he live again ? " arguing from the phenomena of Spiritualism that there was clear proof of the actuality of "the future life." In the evening the same lady treated the subject " Is Spiritualism a religion?" eniphatically declaring the affirmative. At both services Mrs. Newton gave some good clair- voyance. Additional interest was lent to the even- ing by a beautiful poem, " The singing of Magnifi- cat,' being recited by Mr. Frank Linda, of the "Sledge Hammer "Company, in a manner which indicated great elocutionary skill.

Letter s to the Mitou. A DOG OWNER CHALLENGES THE WARRINGTuN POLICE.

Sit,—Would you kindly allow me a little space in your paper to state a little incident in a case taken out by the Warrington police. Last week some toys were playing with a dog when one of them got bitten. The police, believing the dog wis mine, came to my house and demanded my dog,saying they had orders to destroy it—which they did in very short notice. Since then I have discovered that the dog that bit the lad, is still at lar4e and is also without a muzzle. I was also fined I half a crown for my dog not having a muzzle on. I will undertake to present a•sy institution with a £l, if the police can prove that my do r was without a muzzle on the d4tr. tho 1-td ant bitten. Trusting this will meet the eye of Mr. Talbot, lam, yours SO. JOSEPH BENNETT, 68, Battersby-lane.

Three London police officers had a desperate stauggle with an alleged burg Car on Tuesday night. The man had been rushed into a cab with a "one-wrist handcuff " upon him. but with the disengaged hand he managed to fire several shots at his captors and to hit two, one of the officers, Con- stable Brooks, being seriously injured. Harry Aspinall, aged eight, whose parents live in Thomas- street, Crewe, was drowned in a pit at Sidney, near Crewe on Sunday. The pit is small in circumference, but deep. A I men in the neighbouring house heard cries, and golig to the place jumped into the pit. He. however, became embedded in the mud, sinking so deep that onlyihis head was visible. The Bishop of Salford, taking part in a Catholic ceremony at Levenshulme on Saturday, dealt at length with the Catholic position in regard to education. He said that in a country where there were many creeds denominational education was the only system which guarded parenl.al rights, allowed complete freedom of religious tea.ching, and secured perfect equality without. any unworthy compromise or the violation of the rights of conscience. . .

Paper flooring is coming Into favour with German builders.. It is applied in the shape of a layer of pulp. 113's is spread evenly over concrete or boarded foundation, stamped and rolled hard.and painted or dyed In 'mit-Alan of parquetry or momaic work. By using any of the usual flreprosling chetni- cils,the flooring be Names considerably safer in this respect than wood. it Is stated to be warmer for the feet than linoteum,and, as it gives a surfam; without cracks from wall to wall, it is more easily kept clean than are boards. The steamer Benhol rn, of Li verpool. and the Antwerp steamer Klondike collided off Point Lynes, on the Anglesey coast, early on Sunday morning. The Benholm sank, and: nine of her crew and a stowaway were drowned. Ele.mn of I the crew managed to support themselves on flsating pieces of timber until picked up by a tug which at the time of the collision happened to be in the neighbourhood. They were afterwards landed at Liverpool. The Klondike, which was badly damaged, steamed away and succeeded in roaching the Mersey. A violent shock of earthquake occurred on Saturday in the district lying south of Mount Etna. the oscillations extending as far as Mineo and Oallagirone. The walls of several build- ings at Biancavilla were cracked, and at Bempasso several old houses were badly damaged. Several slighter shocks suc- ceeded the first. A telegram from Catania states that earth- quake shocks were experienced in the whole district. At Santa Maria and Nicadia a number of laiuses collapsed. Several churches are so seriously damaged that they have had to be c osed. No loss of life is reported, but a panic prevails among the population. The " Gardeners' Magazine" regards the prospeats of this year's fruit crop as distinctly hopeful. In the more southern counties the apple trees are already in bloom ; but. unlike those of the pears, the plums' and the cherries, the flowers were not sufficiently developed to expose them t 3 the ken easterly winds and sharp frosts that did considerable damage In some districts to other fruits. There is not only a g eater profusion of blossom this year of the whole of our orchard fruits, but the flower* are more perfectly developed than was the case in the spring of 1897, arid therefore are better ahle to I withstand the adverse Influences to which fruits grown in the open in the United Kingdom are exposed. The Duke oi Norfolk, Postmaster-General, presided at a I smoking concert given by the members of the London Postal Football League. In presenting the " Norfolk " cup to the '• Excelsior" (11.P.0 ) football team, the Chairman stated that Englishmen rightly thought much of play, but it should I not occu, y all their thought. Sometimes they might be I called upon to fight, and one of the best methods of keeping I themselves In condition for any kind of fray was practice of the game of football. Ile hoped that, no matter what duties Englishmen were called on to perform, they would continue to keep uo the physical and moral stamina of the great race to which they belonged. At last tr.e truth is out regarding Sir William Crookes's experiments to see if he could substantiate Dr. Bmmens's claim of the transmutation of silver into gold. In his last experiment he assayed a large number of Mexican dollars. and found among them two which were free from gold. Discs were cut from the centre of each, and these two discs were treated as directed by Dr. Emmens in private letters for nearly 100 hours. The resul of this attempt to elange silver Into gold was an utter and blank failure. Dr. Emmons has since written to Sir William about the experiment,giving many reasons why it should not have been successful ; but the Bnglish chemist has announced his intention of making no more attempts at transmutation. _ .

Early on Tuesday a shocking fat;labcident occurred on the new waterworks belonging to the Sheffield Corporation. now in course of construetion at Midhope, near Penistone. At six o'clock five men, who had been working on the night shift—Joshua Roberts of 'fhurlstone, Samuel Lilley,E. Addy, Ernest Blake, avid John Price. all of Langsett, were being drawn out of the valve shaft by a steam crane in charge of William Booth. When the skip In which they were riding reaceed th- surface Booth proceeded to take steps to land them on the platform, when the skip by some means became detached, and all five were precipitated to the bottom a dis- tance of 50 feet. It was found that Roberts (who woe 26 years of age) was mortally injured, and he died about an hour after the accident. Addy's back is broken. Bake is also seriously Injured, and lies in a critical state. Lilley and Price escaped with less severe injuries.

THE LANCASHIRE HUSSARS AT SOUTHPORT—LOCAL

Never before in the annals of Newton, have the chroniclers been able to record the visit of Royalty to their ancient township. The present generation, ' however, will be able to recount to their children the fact that such an honour was conferred upon the village during their lifetime. Though the Royal visit was only a passing one, and occupied the space of not more than five short minutes, yet His Royal Highness the Prince of Wales must have been extremely gratified at the pains taken and the preparations made to display the loyalty which the inhabitants of this part of the empire felt for the Heir Apparent to the English throne. It has been known for some time past that would pass through Newton on his way to Garswood Hall, the residence of Lord Gerrard, the object of the visit being the inspection on Wednesday of the Lanca- shire Hussars, of which Lord Gerrard is the Hon. Colonel. The Urban District Council of Newton-in- Makerfield determined to mark their sense of the high honour conferred upon the township, by pre• seating to 11.R.1.1. an address of welcome. Accordingly a resolution of the Couucil was obtained empowering the expenditure of a certain sum of money in decorations and in the purchase of a suitable address. A committee Was appointed and the result of their labours was seen on Tueselty evening, when the exits from the point of depertun were gay with bunting and streamers. The London and North-Western Railway Company pr f pared the station platform with a length of carpet and down the steps to the exit. Tee rough slope leading into Mill-lame was completely metamorphosed, and tiers of steps covered with red cloth were put down. I temporary covered way was erected, and this being decorated with bunting of bright colours, with plants in full bleom all the way down presented a very pretty effect. At the bottom was a covered platform, and here were assembled the following mem- bers of the District Council :—Messrs. C. B. F. Herron, (chairman). W. Coiling wood, R. Stone, F . °reamed, E. T. Evans, W. Thompson, W. Beatty, J: Simpson, P. isleMehon, W Gilmour,J. H. Forster, F. J. Wood, J. Slee and Dr. Pstterson with, air. W. W. Shirley (clerk) and Mr. R. Brierley (surveyor). Oa a large platform were gathered a number of invited guests, and at the top of the slope was another platform also containing invited guests. Over the County bridge was the motto "Wel- come to Newton-in-Makerfield," and near the Cnurch was the motto " Blessings en our illustrious Prince." At the station approach Was p wee a guard of henour con- sisting of the Newton detachment of the 6th Y.B. K L.R and regimental and bugle band, and in Mill-lane oppo- site the covered platform was a guard composed of a de- tachment of the lit S.L. Prince of Wales volunteers, with the regimental hand from Warrington under the com- mand of Captain and El in. hi ej ir Buckton, the other offisers on parade being 2nd Lieuts, Cr esfield and B A-- nerd. Tne line of route was kept by both regiments, the total number on parade being 200 rank and file, the whole being under the command of Colonel Woad, 6th V.B , the otner officers present being Captain and Adjutant Rowe, and Dr. Harden, acting chaplain. There were also about 70 police, with seven mounted police on the line of route, the whole being in charge of Colonel Moorsom, Chief Constable of Lancashire, with Superintendent Higginbottom of Leigh and several Inspectors. Thera were several thousand people present, numbers being broueht in 'buses and other conveyances from the outlying districts, bet the proceedings throughout were most orderly. EI.R H. left London by a special train at 2-25, Mr. R. Turnbull. superintendent of the line, being in charge of the train, and arrived at Newton-le-Willows punctually at 8 30. Shortly before that time Lord Gerrard's car- riages drove up, and after 140310 conversation between Lord Gerrard and Colonel Wood, his lordship proceeded to the platform to meet the Prince. The Prince of Wales, who arrived at Garswood Hall on Tuesd^y night as the guest of Lord Gerrard, left Brynn Station for Southport at a quarter-past ten yester- day inoening. After luncheon at the Cambridge Hall, w,!ich was attended by many illustrious guests, the Prince planted a tree as a memento of his visit. Subse- quently he visited Wigan, where he was also presented with an address.

COMPLIMENT TO THE WARRINGTON VOLUNTEERS. Tie Warrington Volunteers had the honour of furnish- , ing a guard or honour, at Newton Bridge Station on the arrival of the Prince. They travelled by special train from Rink Quay Station at five o'clock, and on arrival at the station were joined by the Newton contingent, Majer Buckton being in command, accompanied by Second Lieutenant Crosfield and second Lieutenant B.9rnard and 100 rank and file. The regimental band %VW in attendance, under the direction of Bandmaster Armstrong. The smart appearance of the men was favourably commented upon. On their return to the Drill Hall, at Warrington, the men were addressed by Captain and Adjutant F. Mclver Racine, who highly commended them upon their smart, clean, and soldierly appearance, and said they bad acquitted themselves in a manner worthy of the best traditions of the regiment of which they were so proud. It was appropriate that they should have formed a guard of honour to the Prince since the regiment bore his name.

The usual May fair for the sale of horses was held at Newton an Tuesday. Rain fell heavily all day, but in spite of this there was a fool attendance of cattle and prospective buyers. Light horses realised £lB to £25, cart horses were not very glad in class but brought from .£25 to £35. Second-hand aged cobs brought from .E 8 to £l4. Owing to the Royal visit the fair had to close somewhat earlier, which would no doubt to a certain exteas interfere with the sales.

THE DEATH OF SIR, GLADSTONE.

The news of the death of the venerable statesman although not unexpected, was received in Earles- town and district with every manifestation of sor- row. The telegram announcing the sad tidings was received shortly after six o'clock on Thursday morn- ing, and betore breakfast time the flags which were flying at the Conservative Club, Town flail, and other places in honour of the visit of the Prince of Wales, were lowered half mast. There are still some people in Newton who remember Mr. Gladstone's visit to that village, when he contested South West Lancashire for Parliamentary honours.

_ SUDDEN DRATII AT EARLESTOWN. The police have had reported to them the death of Peter Benson, a collier of Dickenson-lane, West- houghton. It appears deceased was on a visit to his brother's house at Newton Cnamon on Friday night. Another man named Sanderford stayed un- til 2.30 a.m. with Benson who then seemed in his usual health. A little later he was found dead. In- formation was given to the police and particulars of the death were forwarded to Hr. Brighouse who decided not to hold an inquest, as the cause of death, certified by the doctor, was apoplexy. De- ceased had beensubject to fits for nine than 28 years. DEATH FROM EXCESSIVE DRINKING A.T EaULES• TOWN.

At the New Market Inn, Zarlestown, on Tuesday evening, Mr. S. Brighouse, coronPr, held an inquiry into the circumstances attending the death of Hannah Melling, 55 years of age, of Peel-street, Earlest-iwn. From the evidence it appeared the deceased had been a heavy drinker for many years, and on Saturday night her husband found her in the hen-cote in the yard. He carried 11r into the house and laid her on the rug in front of the fire, provid- ing a pillow for her head. He afterwards went to bed, and on coming downstairs next morning about 7 o'clock, found her dead where he had laid her. Her daughter spoke to her after Mr. Melling had gone to bed. Dr. Valentine was sent for, but the woman had then been deed some time,and he stated that death had resulted from syncope brought on by excessive drinking.—Verdict accordingly. TRH VICAR OF BIIRTONWJOD AND NRWTON RACES. The Rev. A. H, Mitchell, of Buttonwood, Newton- le-Willows, has addressed the following letter to the chairman of the Burtonwoed School Beard : "Sir,—l am greatly surprised to learn that the board school children have been granted a holiday to-day, Newton Race Day. Your blinl must know quite as well as Ido the extent to which drunken- ness and gambling prevail in this parish, and how Persistently the Church has been endeavouring to counteract the bad habits of the people. The edu- cational authority in a district like this has more than ordinary responsibility laid upon it, because of the evil influences which are at work here among US. and I can but deeoly deplore that your board has, by granting a holiday on this particular day, Shown on which side your sympathies lie, and that, not altogether unwittingly, it has lent itself to the demoralisation of the children committed to its care Walking day in Warrington was originally institu- ted to counteract the attractions of the racecourse in the holiday season, and the procession was until recently always held on the Friday of the 'Newton Meeting. Instead of following so good an example i the beard grants the children eholiday,because tis Newton Race Day I I am quite sure that any re- monstrance from those who are seeking to lift the people up will be of little avail. It is,however, nay duty to protest against your action, and to send copies of this letter to the Education Department and the Press." _

NOTES BY TIR; WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

[BY AN OCCASIONAL _CO RRESPON DENT. 1 The all absorbing topic this week has been the passage through the district of Hie Royal Highness the Prince of Wales. Such an event put everybody on tha tip-toe of expectation,and when the weather on Monday appeared to have settled down and to be on its best behaviour, it was thought there would be a vast gathering of loyal subjects at New- ton to extend a hearty welcome to the Prince. The District Council did their best to make the affair, a succesq,but the disturbing elements cast a damper over the proceedings, and instead of bright sun- shine which characterised the previous day,a leaden sky, and pitiless rain contributed to make every- body feel as miserable as could well be imagined. The decorations were very pretty, and the Prince was heard to remark upon them to Sir Evelyn Wood, an observation which must go some way to make the District Council feel proud of their efforts. The local photographer who intended obtaining one or two views of the ceremony was a greatly disappointed man, as owing to the bad light and other circumstances he was unable to get any sort of a picture.Mr.Borron the chairman of the Council was honoured by an invitation to dine with the Prince and the Hussar officers at Southport.

Many of the inhabitants seconded the efforts of the Council in the way of decoration, and conse- quently there was a plentiful supply of bunting, which no doubt would have looked exceedingly well had the weather been bright. One patriotic publi- can in Newton had erected over his front entrance the photos of the Prince and Princess, and had pro- duced a pretty effect by means of a border of coloured lights. The crowd was a most orderly one, but the cheering WA4 of a feeble character. Perhaps the people were too much overcome by the magnitude of tho honour conferred on the township by a visit from the Heir Apparent to the throne of Great Britain.

At last the Council have decided to erect a mor- tuary Lir the reception of any badies for which the police cannot find a suitable resting place pending identiLlation.. I have,in these columns,aten advo- cated the provision of such a building,and naturally am proud that the Council have at last seen their way to proceed with the erection of the much needed building. I hope they will see that suita- ble apparatus is proviued for the conducting of post-mortem examinations, and that sufficient ac- commodation is made for the storage of an ambul- ance.

The question of obtaining an independent sup- ply of water for the township is being proceeded with. An engineer has been retained at a fixed rernuneration,and the sub-committee has been ern- powered to bore to a depth of 500 feet in search of , the needed liquid. All this is very well in its way, but what of the warning administered by one of the colliery firms,who it is said will,in the not very distant future, sink a pit from which they purpose obtaining coal, Should this be done, it is not very assuring to think what will become of the Council's water. It would be a pity to waste money in the face of this warning,and it is to be hoped the Coun- cil will weigh the matter well before proceeding With the scheme.

NEWTON RACES iTH THEIR SEQUEL.

One of the most prolific sources of police court Mee is the race course and its consequent betting Iran- sactions,a hieh with men ei doubtful character so often end in that class of offence commonly called "welshinc,r." The cliff lrenc class of offal:piers and the particular class of offence in which a certain stamp of men who at- tend the races so often overstep the fine line which di- vides the spirit from the letter of the law are too numer- ous to particularise. The race course public generally are only to ready to take summary vengeance on that particular class of tricksters styled by theca as " welshers." These " sharps," who endeavour to escape their honourable,and on the racecourse, legal debts, are thankful to have the protection of the police from the , physical violence of the public, when caught. The fol- lowing Cases at the Warring,ton County Petty Seesions are examples of some of the off-mcee attendant on the different race meetings throughout the country : AN ALLEGED WELEGIER.—Charies Smethurst. was charged with having obtained on the 13th May at Newton, unlawfully and by means of a trick, the sum of 10e.—The prisoner plcadrd not Waddicor stated that at 2.15 p.m. on Friday last the prisoner was given In charge at Newton Race Course for "welshing." FELONE.—Henry Platt and William Jona' were charged with being on the railway station at Barlestown on the 13th May with latest to commit a felony, Both prisoners pleaded not guilty. Mr. Fenno prosecuted on behalf of the L. and N.W. Railway Company. Mr. Hyslop appeared for the defenee.Mr.Fenna called Walter Kynaston.a detective officer in the employ of the L and N W Railway Company, who said he saw the two defendants on the station in company with two other men not In custody. This was about 5 o'clock. They crossed over to the other platform and had & conversation together. The four then separated and Platt rind Jones got together again. He watched the two knocking about in a suspicious way. An excursion train came in, and there was a crowd of people trying to get into the train. He heard Jones say there is nothing on this train we will wait for the next." Later on he saw one of them put his hand into a pocket—George Jones, also a detective in the employ of the London and North- Western Railway Co., corroborated in effect the statements of the previous officer. He had seen Platt put his hand in the pocket of a lady covered by the prisoner Jones.—P.O. Duncan, stationed at Leigh. said he was on the station at 5 o'clock. He saw the prisoner Platt, put his hand In several ladies pockets as they were getting lido the train. His operations were covered by Jones. When prisoner Platt was searched he had 7s. 11 in money but no railway ticket.— P.C. Carter, said he was on daty at Bariestown station on the day in question.—Kynaston made a communication to him, and pointed out the two prisoners. When searched Jones had 21 17s. Id., a watch and gold chain, a race card, and a return half ticket to Rdgehill. He said he came from ' Wolverhampton. Platt said he earns from Liverpool.—Mr. slop, for the defence, said as far as the prisoner Jones was concerned, there was no case agaiLst him. He was there for a lawful purpose. There was a race card found on him, and that, ne said, showed for what purpose he had been there. The prisoners had said they were looking on the station and in the trains for a man, and he submitted that this was a case In which the Scotch verdict might come In "not proven."— Walter K 3 naston, on oath, said he was present at Salford in 1896 when the prisoner Platt got three months for falling to report himself to the police.—The magistrates sent each of the prisoners to gaol for three months with hard labour.

Haddock (gas engineer), Mr. John Sutcliffe (assistant borough surveyor), Mr. W.H.
