STREET IMPROTEMENTS.

In answer to the Inspector, the Town Clerk stated that the population of the borough at the last cens is was 52542; it was now estimated at 63,000. The borough was extended in 1896. The Borough Treasurer stated that the assessable value of the borough was £186,897. Thc palm" of out- standing loans undrr the Sanitary and Public Elealth Acts amounted to £72,369.

The Inspector What period do you ask for the loan? The Town Clerk : Fifty years for the land, and we suggest 30 years for street paving and se on. The Inspector : Is there anyone present who wishes to oppose the application for the borrowing of £31,99 3 for street improvements ? No one objected. The Town Clerk explained the proposals of the Cor- poration and obaerved that Warrington was intersected by four very narrow streete—a legacy from their fore- fathers—and they found it absolutely necessary to ' widen them. The traffic was very rapidly increasinz, and two applications had been made to the Local Government Beard for leave to construct tramways in the borough, but each application had been refused by the Board of Trade. The applications were not made by the Corporation, but by private companies. At the end of Bridge-street there was a bridge over the river Mersey, which was the first bridge for vehicular traffic, between Liverpool and Warrington, and was the only means of communication for many miles round between the two counties. All the traffic came up Bridge-street —or most of it—and had to go through the narrow streets. The Borough Surveyor had estimated that 3.500 vehicles passed up and down Bridge-street each I day. He (Mr. Whittle) was pleaeed to say that the ' town was never its a more prosperous condition than it was at the present time. The traffic was increasing daily, and there were occasions when 83020 of the streets,, the principle ones, were almoet it:wags- able, particularly on Saturday, when there was great difficulty in getting down Bridge-street. The Ex-Mayor (Alderman James Fairclough) had taken upon himself the grave responsibility of making certain purchases iu his own name for the Corporation. They had an agreement with him by which he undertook te sell to the Corporation. He had purchased all the pro- perty which was wauted with two exceptions. He en- tered into an agreement with the Corporation to sell at the same price he paid for the preperties,the Corporation indemnifying him against any loss. Thd Corporation had paid part of the purchase money of same strips of land along Wilderspool Causeway. The land had been acquired to widen an old county road. It was net a main road. The Corporation had now taken the road over. It was a very narrow roadway. Most of the land belonged to Messrs. Greetiall, Whitley and Company, who were brewers in the borough, and it was eligible building and, and about to be brought into the market for building purposes. There was no footpath on one side of the road. On one side of the road was public- house, but this it was proposed to pull down and erect another one further back. The Corporation had suffi- cient land to widen the road to 80ft. They had agreed to glee £5OO towards the cast of rebuilding the premises which, he believed, would coat £2,000. The owners of the land had met the Corporation in a very liberal spirit. They had widened the road to 40ft, without any charge to the Corporation, and had sold the remainder of the land sufficient to widened to 601 t. The road had only an average of 25ft. in width ; and the Curporation had increased that to 80ft-, for which they had paid 61. per square yard, freehold. All the property they were purchasing was freehold ; and free from eucumberances with the exception of Queen's Garden they did not believe in leasehold pro- perty. The traffic along the Causeway was immense ; the road was about a mile in length. He wished to make an explanation about the proposed improvement in Knutsford-road. It was another ancient causeway, and very narrow, with a footpath on only one side part of the way. They required to purchase strips of land to make the road 60ft. wide. Land had been purchased by the Deputy-Mayor in trust for the town during his mayoralty. In connection with Victoria Park, it waa proposed to construct a roadway from Knutsford-road across the estate, and to pass over the river by a new bridge to connect that part of the town with Latchford. The Corporation bad not purchased the estate • it was in the possession of Alderman James Fairclough, on the ' same conditions as before. They proposed to take land from Mr. Fairclough at the front at the same price they paid to Greenall, Whitley, and Co., namely fis. per yard. They had bought certain land in St. James's-street to widen the street. It had not yet been paid for, and the negotiations ware not complete el when they approached the Local Government Board. The price to be paid was 15s. a equate yard. which would come to £999 7s. Bd., and £BOO for buildings. _ .

Mr. T. Longdin, borough surveyor, in answer to the Inspector, said all the streets were sewered and the sewerage was discharged into the river. They were on the pail system and there were not more than 500 water closets in the town. There was a very excessive amount of water poured by the manufacturers into the sewers. The paving works were similar in all cases except in the new street proposed from Bank-street to Cannell-street, and the only difference there was the size of sett. The foundation of the streets was composed of sin. concrete and paved with "63 "granite setts. The kerbs pro- posed were 12 by Rawtenstall kerbs, and the foc,tpath inch concrete flags. The width of the streets 9i:cord- ing to the bye-laws was 40 feet. An allowance was made for the cost of kerbs which would be used again in set- ting back.

===== 0002601_18980520_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
SLOT AND OTHER MITIOIB,

The next inquiry was with respect to the slot and other meters. The Town Clerk said the Warrington Gas Works were bought in 1877,and they they now asked for the loan— for not more than 15 years—of £lO,OOO for the purchase of slot and other meters. The Corporation had already spent out of revenue £6,600 for slot meters, but that was not included in the £lO,OOO, and the Gas Commit- tee had been called over the coals for spending its revenue in this lavish manner. The Chairman of the Finance Committee thoueht he was entitled to it for a reduction of the rates, Their gas engineer estimated that in ten years he would require 5,000 meters at £2 each. This year he would require 1,000. They found the slot meter an exceedingly popular method of supply- ing poor people with gas. It was the penny in the slot system. 'they had at the present moment no fewer than 100 applications on the books for these meters. The Corporation bad not borrowed money before for the purchase of the slot meters. They had borrowed £20,000 for extensions, £5,000 of which was vet un- spent. The Gas Works were the property of the Cor- poration, and were acquired in 1877. They had been greatly enlarged since that date, out of revenue mainly, and new works had been practically built out of revenue. The Corporation wished to take up the loan as it was required. Mr. W. S. Haddock, stated thet the yearly consump- tion of gas at the works was 216 million of feet from the ordinary meters, and 20-i million feet from the slot meters. The works could turn out one million feet per day. There were 3,200 slot meters in use at the present time. The Corporation fixed the meters, and the rent etc., was all covered by the coin. There was 25 ft. of gas obtained for a penny, and this would burn 7 hours for one light. The Corporatien got a five years guarantee with each meter. If this loan was secured they would encourage the fixing of these meters. They were first used in Warrington in 1896, and were almost wnolly used by the working classes. Alderman Sutton : I should like to know if any cal- culation has been made as to the profits of the slot meters?

Witness: We have made a calculation and it is satis• Factory.

The Town Clerk : We believe we get less profit from the slot rneters,but it is an encouragement to the people to use gas.

The Deputy-Mayor: They get 251 t for a penny. QUEEN'S GARDENS. The next part of the inquiry related to the £7OO for the purchase and laying out of Queen's Gardens. The Town Clerk said the Corporation asked for a loan for 50 years for the land and for the laying out ten years. The gardens were nearly an acre in extent and were situated almost in the centre of the town and formerly were divided into eight small gardens and rented by the persons who lived near them. The land was offered for sale by auction, and was secured by the Corporation for £2OO in 1897. The land was freehold, and there was only one condition attached to it, and that was that it should never be built upon. It bad a good access, and was close to several Of the public buildings of the town. Mr. Longdin bad estimated that it would cost £5OO to lay out the land. The Inspector (looking at the plan) What is the space in the centre left for ? The Borough Surveyor : We expect some gentleman to present us with a handsome Luntain some day. (Laughter.) The inquiry then terminated, and shortly afterwards the Inspector left the Town Hall for the purpose of view- ing the sites in question.

BOMBARDMENT OF SANTIAGO DE CUBA. MADRID, THURSDAY. Three Arnericah warships yesterday bombarded the fort of Santiagode Cuba, but without doing any damage. the shells falling short of laud owing to the long rango from which they were fired. They fired altegethet about eighty shots of shell. The Spanish batteries re- plied to the fire of the attacking warships and damaged one of them. Half an hour later the French despatch boat Fulton entered the harbour,

t plied

Supplies of proviaions in Cuba are stated in a private telegram to be abundant, in spite of the blockade, as vessels with food supplies are continually arriving. A report is current that Admiral Grvera's squadron has arrived off Havana, but th,, rumour is act yet cod' firmed.
